Two-dimensional transducers
Fosco Loregian

Abstract
. We define a bicategory 𝟐 ​ 𝐓 ​ 𝐃 ​ 𝐗 \mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX} whose 1-cells provide a categorification of transducers , computational devices extending finite-state automata with output capabilities. This bicategory is a mathematically interesting object: its objects are categories 𝒜 , ℬ , … \mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\dots and its 1-cells ( 𝒬 , t ) : 𝒜 ℬ (\mathcal{Q},t):\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 6.82854pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.53581pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 17.07164pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 17.07164pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{B} consist of a category 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} of ‘states’, and a profunctor t : 𝒜 × 𝒬 op × 𝒬 × ( ℬ ∗ ) op \textstyle{t:\mathcal{A}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\times(\mathcal{B}^{*})^{\mathrm{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces} 𝐒𝐞𝐭 \textstyle{\mathbf{Set}} where ℬ ∗ \mathcal{B}^{*} denotes the free monoidal category over ℬ \mathcal{B} . Extending t t to 𝒜 ∗ \mathcal{A}^{*} in a canonical way, to each ‘word’ a ¯ \underline{a} in 𝒜 ∗ \mathcal{A}^{*} one attaches an endoprofunctor over the category 𝒬 \mathcal{Q} of states, enriched over presheaves on ℬ ∗ \mathcal{B}^{*} . We discuss a number of other characterizations of the hom-category 𝟐 ​ 𝐓 ​ 𝐃 ​ 𝐗 ​ ( 𝒜 , ℬ ) \mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}) ; we establish a Kleisli-like universal property for 𝟐 ​ 𝐓 ​ 𝐃 ​ 𝐗 ​ ( 𝒜 , ℬ ) \mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}) and explore the connection of 𝟐 ​ 𝐓 ​ 𝐃 ​ 𝐗 \mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX} to other bicategories of computational models, such as Bob Walters’ bicategory of ‘circuits’; it is convenient to regard 𝟐 ​ 𝐓 ​ 𝐃 ​ 𝐗 \mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX} as the loose bicategory of a double category 𝔻 ​ 𝐓𝐃𝐗 \mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX} : the bicategory (resp., double category) of profunctors is naturally contained in the bicategory (resp., double category) 𝟐 ​ 𝐓 ​ 𝐃 ​ 𝐗 \mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX} (resp., 𝔻 ​ 𝐓𝐃𝐗 \mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX} ); we study the completeness and cocompleteness properties of 𝔻 ​ 𝐓𝐃𝐗 \mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX} , the existence of companions and conjoints, and we sketch how monads, adjunctions, and other structures/properties that naturally arise from the definition work in 𝔻 ​ 𝐓𝐃𝐗 \mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX} .



1.
Introduction

This paper analyzes the properties of a class of computational machines known as
transducers
—an important concept in theoretical computer science, especially in the study of formal languages and automata theory— from a category-theoretic standpoint. Our perspective is that of a
categorification
, building on the classical notion long studied by computer scientists. In this sense, the present work lives within a line of research (cf.
[
KR90
,
KKR83
,
BK82
,
BK81
]
,
[
Gui80
,
Gui78
,
Gui74
]
, and
[
KSW02
]
) that the author has been attempting to revive for the last few years (cf.
[
BLLL23a
,
BLLL23b
,
Lor24
,
Lor25
]
), advocating the use of
formal category theory
as a foundational framework for automata theory.

This perspective reveals intriguing patterns, both for the category theorist — who may discover concrete instances of concepts traditionally viewed as highly abstract by other mathematicians — and for the automata theorist — who may gain access to a powerful, systematic framework for compositional properties of their familiar structures of interest.

Paré has contributed to this part of category theory with his elegant paper
[
Par10
]
on ‘Mealy morphisms’ between enriched categories. There, he showed that by abstracting appropriately from the standard mathematical definition of a Mealy automaton, one arrives at a bicategory in which the 1-cells occupy a position intermediate between enriched functors and enriched profunctors. More indirectly, Paré’s work embodies an aesthetic perspective that resonates strongly with the author, which is the idea that a mathematician should never shy away from asking seemingly naïve questions (or even ‘two silly ones’, as in
[
Par21b
]
), since the answers may reveal unexpected analogies.

Here, the ‘silly question’ we seek to address is:

How to convince a mathematician that a transducer is an interesting object to study, aided and motivated by category theory?

In the pursuit of a (hopefully) satisfactory answer, the paper also engages with several other areas of category theory explored by Paré, including but not limited to the theory of profunctors, double categories
[
GP99
]
, categories enriched over presheaves
[
Woo76
]
, and graded monads, which he touched in
[
Par21a
]
. In our account, a 2-transducer emerges as a 1-cell in a bicategory
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
(or better, a loose morphism in a double category
\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}
) of ‘graded-and-indexed’ profunctors. The aim of the present work is to make this idea precise, and explore the properties of
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
and
\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}
.
1
1

1

This notational convention will be implicitly assumed to distinguish categories and 2-categories (bold) from double categories (bbold and bold).

Having drawn constant inspiration and insight from Paré’s work, it is with great pleasure and gratitude that we submit this work for consideration.

1.1.
Motivation, background and the idea behind transducers

Let’s now turn to the mathematics, starting with a brief introduction on the classical notion of transducer, and how it can be seen as a profunctor.

Shortly put, transducers are computational devices that extend finite-state automata with the ability to produce output; as such, they can be thought of as
translation machines
, or converters, between different languages: at the bare essential, a finite word
\underline{a}=a_{1}a_{2}\dots a_{n}
(say, in an alphabet
A
) goes in, a computation
t
happens, and a set of finite words
t(\underline{a})
(in a possibly different alphabet
B
) goes out. When such set is a singleton,
t
is called
deterministic
.

So a transducer
t
can be thought of as a finite-state machine
equipped with a dictionary
: for each transition
x\xrightarrow{\underline{a}}x^{\prime}
between states induced by the word
\underline{a}
,
t
produces a corresponding (set of) output(s). As such, transducers can serve a myriad of purposes, such as models for string-processing systems —simple character encoders or complex signal processors– and they find widespread application in many areas.

State-of-the-art research has already attempted to study transducers through a category-theoretic lens, for example using tools from the theory of coalgebras
[
Han10
,
AMV11
]
. The theory lends itself to many generalizations and variations, such as
weighted

[
MPR01
]
or
probabilistic

[
Eis02
]
transducers.

Here we take a deeper category-theoretic stance, motivated by the fact that (despite their broad applicability and interest from the community of algebraists, see e.g.
[
Cho04
]
), the existing literature does not seem to expand on the idea that, as dictionaries (and more importantly, databases, cf.
[
SSVW17
]
) can be fruitfully thought as profunctors, so should be transducers: given alphabets
A,B
, a profunctor from
A^{*}
to
B^{*}
assigns to each word
(w,w^{\prime}):A^{*}\times B^{*}
a set
p(w,w^{\prime})
, which can be thought of as the set of all ways one can effectively translate
w
into
w^{\prime}
passing from
A
to
B
.

The fact that transducers admit a compositional structure (translation can happen in series) and a monoidal structure (translation can, at least in principle, happen in parallel) is mentioned by many (cf. for example
[
Moh04
, §3]
) but isn’t made into a precise assertion of compositionality/monoidality of a category of which transducers are arrows.

In this work we take the above analogy ‘profunctors
\approx
dictionaries’ seriously, and we aim to describe said compositional and monoidal structure. The answer we find unveils several analogies, requiring us to dive into delightfully sophisticated category theory.

To attain this goal, the classical notion of (1-)transducer is first categorified, and transducers are seen as 1-cells of a bicategory (or more conveniently, as loose morphisms of a double category)
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
of
2-transducers
; then a bicategory of (1-)transducers can be seen as made by 2-transducers between discrete categories, and having discrete state categories. We then turn to explore various properties of
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
,
qua
bicategory and horizontal bicategory of a double category
\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}
; in particular, we discuss several universal constructions, as well as monads and adjunctions in
\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}
, and the relation of
\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}
with other models for computational (‘Mealy’) machines.

Now that the plan has been disclosed, let’s start at the beginning. The idea of a (nondeterministic) transducer as a dictionary is mathematically captured defining it as a tuple
T=(Q,A,B,t)
where

td
1)

Q
is a set of
states
(often called a state ‘space’ even when no topology whatsoever is involved),

td
2)

A
is the
input
alphabet (on which we consider the set of words
A^{*}
),

td
3)

B
is the
output
alphabet (on which we consider the set of words
B^{*}
),

td
4)

t\subseteq A\times Q\times Q\times B^{*}
is a
transition relation
, specified so that there is a unique extension
\bar{t}
to a relation
A^{*}\times Q\times Q\times B^{*}
.
2
2

2

More than often,
Q,A,B
are
finite
sets; from a purely mathematical point of view, this restriction is a bit unnatural —all the more because at least
some
infinitary constructions are needed to make the theory expressive enough. There is a certain amount of work in re-enacting automata theory in minimal assumptions, such as ‘an elementary topos with a NNO’, cf
[
Iwa24
]
. Grothendieck toposes offer a better framework, and it has been proposed that they should be the right setting to subsume ‘the technical details of automata theory’, cf.
[
Hor24
,
BFL+23
]
. In short, this approach is motivated by the general fact that given a well-behaved enough endofunctor
F
on a topos, the
F
-coalgebras form themselves a topos.

As specified in
td4
, the transition relation
t
can be rewritten as a function of type

(1.1)

\textstyle{A\times Q\times Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\text{P}({B^{*}})}

into the powerset of
B^{*}
, which in turn can be interpreted as a function assigning a
Q
-by-
Q
matrix of elements in the semiring
\text{P}({B^{*}})
to each element of
A
, and by extension to each list
w:A^{*}
, so that the matrices
t_{a}
form a
representation
of the free monoid
(A^{*},\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\mathchoice{\mathop{\scalebox{.75}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.99997pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.55}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.99997pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.45}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.99997pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.25}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.99997pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}$},\texttt{[\,]})
into the semiring
\text{Mat}(Q,\text{P}({B^{*}}))
of such
Q
-by-
Q
matrices, i.e. a monoid homomorphism

(1.2)

\textstyle{t:(A^{*},\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\mathchoice{\mathop{\scalebox{.75}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.99997pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.55}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.99997pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.45}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.99997pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.25}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.99997pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}$},\texttt{[\,]})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\text{Mat}(Q,\text{P}({B^{*}})),\circ,\mathrm{Id}_{Q}).}

Let’s consider a very small example, which will clarify the situation to those who meet the notion of transducer for the first time; let
A=\{a,b\},B=\{x\}

denote the alphabets,
Q=\{1,2\}
the state space. A transducer is specified in terms of two 2-by-2 matrices

(1.3)

t(a)=\begin{pmatrix}f_{11}(x)&f_{12}(x)\\
f_{21}(x)&f_{22}(x)\end{pmatrix}\qquad\qquad t(b)=\begin{pmatrix}g_{11}(x)&g_{12}(x)\\
g_{21}(x)&g_{22}(x)\end{pmatrix}

whose entries
f_{ij}(x),g_{ij}(x)
, dependent on a single indeterminate
x
, are understood as characteristic functions
\{x\}^{*}\to\{0,1\}
, belonging to the formal power series ring
\textbf{B}\langle\!\!\langle x\rangle\!\!\rangle
of (noncommutative, but when
B
is a singleton this is not evident) polynomials over the set
B
of indeterminates, and Boolean coefficients.
3
3

3

To each subset
S\subseteq B^{*}
, denoted
S=\{x^{n_{s}}\mid s:S\}
, one associates the series
\sum_{s:S}x^{n_{s}}
; clearly, each Boolean-valued series of this form determines a subset of
B^{*}
as its support. So, the series
0
corresponds to the empty set
\varnothing\subset B^{*}
, the series
x
corresponds to the subset
\{[x]\}
(where
[x]:=x\mathbin{::}\texttt{[\,]}
), the series
x+x^{2}
corresponds to
\{[x],[x,x]\}
, etc.
Just for the sake of concreteness, let’s say
t(a)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&0\\
0&x+x^{2}\end{smallmatrix}\right),t(b)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1&x\\
x&x\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

To such an arrangement one associates a directed graph representing the
dynamics
of
t
, where there is a node for each state
q:Q
and an edge labeled
q\xrightarrow{i/f}q^{\prime}
, decorated by
(i,f):A\times\textbf{B}\langle\!\!\langle x\rangle\!\!\rangle
if and only if
t(i)_{qq^{\prime}}=f
: for the
t(a),t(b)
above, we then have the diagram

(1.4)

1
2
a/1
b/1
a/x+x^{2}
b/x
b/x
b/x

Now, it’s clear how to interpret (
1.4
) in terms of an elementary notion: once a representation
t
of
A^{*}
as (
1.2
) is regarded as a functor
\Sigma(A^{*})\to\mathbf{Set}
out of the monoid
A^{*}
, regarded as a single-object free category
\Sigma A^{*}
, one can turn
t
into an opfibration
P_{t}:\mathcal{E}[t]\to\Sigma(A^{*})
, via the
Grothendieck-Bénabou
(or ‘category of elements’)
construction

[
BS00
, §7]
.

As such,
\mathcal{E}[t]
is the category of elements, or
action category
(by which we mean the non-groupoidal equivalent of action
groupoids
, cf.
[
IR19
, Definition 4.7]
) of
t
, having objects the states
q:Q
and morphisms the
t(a)/f_{qq^{\prime}}:q\to q^{\prime}
above. Given the equivalence of categories between functors
\Sigma(A^{*})\to\mathbf{Set}
and discrete opfibrations over
\Sigma(A^{*})
, the action category faithfully represents the evolution in discrete time of the representation
t
.

It is now an easy observation, first put in print by Walters
[
Wal89
]
, that if
t
is a representation of a free
monoid
, then
\mathcal{E}[t]
is a free
category
: in particular, in our example,

Remark 1.1
.

The action category
\mathcal{E}[t]
associated with the copresheaf
t
of (
1.3
) is free on the graph (
1.4
).

This fibrational approach has a certain established history developed by Walters and extended by others, cf.
[
Wal89
,
MZ23a
,
MZ23b
]
. However, this is just part of the story: every transducer
t
yields a
2\times 2
matrix for each word
w:\{a,b\}^{*}
, uniquely determined as the product of the generating matrices
t(a),t(b)
in (
1.3
); for example, to the words ‘
ab
’ and ‘
ba
’ one associates the product matrices

(1.5)

t(ab)=t(a)t(b)=\begin{pmatrix}1&x\\
x^{2}+x^{3}&x^{2}+x^{3}\end{pmatrix}\qquad t(ba)=t(b)t(a)=\begin{pmatrix}1&x^{2}+x^{3}\\
x&x^{2}+x^{3}\end{pmatrix}

and thus additional edges appear in the above diagram (of which we draw only some, shading in gray the generating edges):
1
2
a/1
b/1
ab/1
a/x+x^{2}
b/x
ba/x^{2}+x^{3}
b/x
b/x
ab/x^{2}+x^{3}

Thus, in addition to being the total space of a discrete opfibration, in the above class of examples the action category
\mathcal{E}[t]
is also
graded
, in the sense that the composition of edges

\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
, labeled by
ab\big/(x+x^{2})\cdot x
; more generally, given a map like (
1.1
) and a word
w=(a_{1},\dots,a_{n}):A^{*}
, we express
\big(t(w)\big)_{ij}
as a product

(1.6)

\big(t(w)\big)_{ij}=\big(t(a_{1})\dots t(a_{n})\big)_{ij}=\sum_{q_{1},\dots,q_{n-1}:Q}t(a_{1})_{i,q_{1}}\cdot t(a_{2})_{q_{1},q_{2}}\cdots t(a_{n})_{q_{n-1},j},

where the sum and the iterated product is understood in terms of the semiring operations.

Categorification (colloquially known as ‘the left adjoint to the process of forgetting category theory’) now provides us with an idea to make this story more appealing for a category theorist: a map like (
1.1
), decomposed as in (
1.6
), is a particular instance of a profunctor

(1.7)

\textstyle{t:\mathcal{A}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}^{(\mathcal{B}^{*})^{\mathrm{op}}}}

when
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{Q}
are discrete categories (regarded as categories enriched over
\textbf{B}=\{\varnothing,\top\}
); in this perspective (
1.6
) expresses the profunctor composition

(1.8)

\textstyle{\mathcal{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t(a_{1})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t(a_{2})}
\textstyle{\dots\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t(a_{n})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Q}.}

So, a transducer can be formally described as a pair
(\mathcal{Q},t)
, consisting of a category and a profunctor as in (
1.7
); viewing
(\mathcal{Q},t)
as a 1-cell
\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 12.33958pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 12.42471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.84944pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.84944pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{B}
, any two such cells
(\mathcal{P},s):\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 12.33958pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 12.42471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.84944pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.84944pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{B}
and
(\mathcal{Q},t):\mathcal{B}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 12.33958pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 12.42471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.84944pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.84944pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{C}
, can be composed to form a 1-cell
(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{P},T\circ s):\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 12.33958pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 12.42471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.84944pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.84944pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{C}
,
4
4

4

The profunctor
T\circ s
of type
\mathcal{A}\times{(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{P})}^{\mathrm{op}}\times(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{P})\times(\mathcal{B}^{*})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Set}
can be defined as a suitable composition of profunctors; what is interesting about this notion of composition, that we spell out in detail in (
2.14
), is that a transducer
(\mathcal{Q},t):\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 12.33958pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 12.42471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.84944pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.84944pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{B}
can be regarded as a profunctor indexed over
\mathcal{A}
, graded over
\mathcal{Q}
, and enriched over
\mathcal{B}
.
thus defining the bicategory
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
, which is the subject of our interest.

Several notions are needed to make this precise, the most important of which is the
grading
expressed by the fact that the category of states of a composite transducer is the product of the categories of states of its components.

We can introduce such grading into the picture in the following way (a perspective that we first learned from Paré’s paper
[
Par21a
]
on ‘morphisms of rings’).
Recall what is known as a
graded monad
(cf.
[
Fuj19
]
for a detailed, modern account; the definition however appears very early in the work of Bénabou):

Definition 1.2

(Graded monad)
.

Let
(\mathcal{M},\otimes,1)
be a monoidal category. An
\mathcal{M}
-graded monad

(T,\eta,\mu)
consists of a lax functor
T:\mathcal{M}\to\mathbf{Cat}
; as such, a graded monad comprises

•

an endofunctor
T_{M}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}
of a category
\mathcal{X}
, for every object
M:\mathcal{M}_{0}
;

•

a graded unit (the
unitor
) i.e. a natural transformation of type

(1.9)

\textstyle{\eta:\text{id}_{\mathcal{X}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T_{1}}

where
1
is the monoidal unit of
\mathcal{M}
;

•

a graded multiplication (the
compositor
), a family of natural transformations of type

(1.10)

\textstyle{\mu_{MM^{\prime}}:T_{M}\circ T_{M^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{T_{M\otimes M^{\prime}},}

subject to associativity and unitality axioms (see
[
Par21a
, Definition 12]
, or
[
Fuj19
, 2.1.1]
, for the explicit definition).

Graded monads appear in our discussion in two (related) ways: first, there is a graded monad of matrices over any fixed commutative (semi)ring
R
, a fact witnessed by how every
p\times p
matrix-of-matrices
\boldsymbol{M}:\text{Mat}_{p}(\text{Mat}_{q}(R))
, each entry of which is a
q\times q
matrix
M_{ij}:\text{Mat}_{q}(R)
, can be flattened to a
pq\times pq
matrix. More precisely,

Remark 1.3

(
[
Par21a
, Proposition 13]
)
.

There is a graded monad
\text{Mat}:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbf{Cat}
from the multiplicative monoid of natural numbers (regarded as a single-object, 2-discrete bicategory), into
\mathbf{Cat}
, picking the category of rings and where each
\text{Mat}_{n}
acts sending a ring
R
to the ring of
n\times n
matrices with entries in
R
. The monad unit consists of the isomorphism
R\to\text{Mat}_{1}(R)\cong R
, and the multiplication is the function ‘flattening’ a matrix of matrices
\boldsymbol{M}
into a matrix of scalars
M
, having as first row the concatenation of first rows of all matrices in the first row of
\boldsymbol{M}
, as second row the concatenation of all second rows of all matrices in the first row…, etc.: if
(p,q)=(2,2)
for example, the multiplication acts as

(1.11)

\begin{bmatrix}\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0&1\\
2&3\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]&\left[\begin{smallmatrix}4&5\\
6&7\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]\\[1.99997pt]
\left[\begin{smallmatrix}8&9\\
10&11\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]&\left[\begin{smallmatrix}12&13\\
14&15\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right]\\
\end{bmatrix}\mapsto\left[\begin{smallmatrix}0&1&4&5\\
2&3&6&7\\
8&9&12&13\\
10&11&14&15\end{smallmatrix}\right]

In a similar fashion, one can resort to a categorified analogue of
Remark 1.3
to compose two 2-transducers by multiplying the grading categories, meaning that the composition of
(\mathcal{P},s):\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.20857pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 36.0113pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 78.02263pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 78.02263pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{B}
with
(\mathcal{Q},t):\mathcal{B}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.20857pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 36.0113pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 78.02263pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 78.02263pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{C}
is of the form
(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{P},\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,):\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.20857pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 36.0113pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 78.02263pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 78.02263pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{C}
.

But in addition to this, graded monads also appear when one considers monads in the bicategory
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
, or better, in the double category
\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}
. For instance, in
Theorem 3.11
we prove the following result.

Theorem
.

A monad
(\mathcal{Q},t):\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.55554pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.20857pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 36.0113pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 78.02263pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 78.02263pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{A}
in
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
consists of a promonad on
\mathcal{A}^{*}
, such that
\mathcal{Q}
acquires a monoidal structure
\boxtimes
, and
t
is graded over
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}
(equipped with the component-wise monoidal structure
(A,B)\boxtimes(C,D):=(A\boxtimes^{\mathrm{op}}C,B\boxtimes D)
induced by
\mathcal{Q}
).

We now give a more detailed account of the results contained in the present work.

1.2.
Outline of the paper

In order to prepare the ground for a categorification of the notion of transducer, we start
section 2
by setting all the classical pieces and definitions in place; this will motivate the need to regard a map like (
1.1
)
as a functor of type

(1.12)

\textstyle{t:\mathcal{A}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle}

where
\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
is a shorthand for the presheaf category over
\mathcal{B}^{*}
;
starting from here, we define the bicategory
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
. This is a kind of structure drawing from diverse parts of category theory, such as classical enriched profunctors
[
Bor94
, 6.2.10]
, and more modern attempts to categorify free semirings,
5
5

5

A rather well-established branch of monoidal category theory
[
KL80
,
Lap72
,
Elg21
,
BMT23
,
BMT24
]
that the author touched upon in
[
LT23
,
Lor24
]
.
and with the theory of graded monads
[
MU22a
,
MU22b
,
GKOS21
,
OWE20
,
Fuj19
,
DMS19
,
FKM16
,
MPS15
]
.

After some preliminaries, in
Definition 2.7
we recall what kind of 1-dimensional structure is a transducer: a certain monoid homomorphism
t:A^{*}\to\text{Mat}(Q,\text{P}({B^{*}}))
for suitable alphabets
A,B
and a finite state space
Q
, where
\text{P}({B^{*}})
denotes the free quantale on
B^{*}
(see
Definition 2.7
). These gadgets are naturally understood as
matrices of formal power series
, and we leverage on this idea in
Definition 2.9
, to define a
2-transducer
as a strong monoidal functor
t:\mathcal{A}^{*}\to\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\text{-}\mathbf{Prof}(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{Q})
, where
\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
is the free cocompletion
[
DL07
]
of the free monoidal category
\mathcal{B}^{*}
on
\mathcal{B}
(this is called the
free cocomplete 2-rig
on
\mathcal{B}
, cf.
[
LT23
]
). Such
t
form the 1-cells of a bicategory
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
.

Furthermore, when
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\dots
are discrete categories, one can think of them as the sub-2-category
\mathbf{TDX}
spanned by the 2-transducers where the image of
t:A\times{Q}^{\mathrm{op}}\times Q\times(B^{*})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Set}
factors through the subcategory
\{\varnothing,\top\}=:\textbf{B}
of truth values; in this case a 2-transducer boils down exactly to a transducer as classically defined –a graded version of profunctor composition defines composition of 1-cells for both
\mathbf{TDX}
and
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
. To the best of our knowledge, the fact that 1-transducers are composable, although obvious, this doesn’t seem to have been explicitly spelled out so far.

The progression in categorification similar to what happens with relations, regarded as special profunctors, and profunctors, regarded as the loose bicategory of a (pseudo) double category:

(1.13)

\textstyle{\mathbf{Rel}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\supseteq

\textstyle{\mathbf{Prof}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\supseteq

\textstyle{\mathbb{P}\mathbf{rof}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\supseteq

\textstyle{\mathbf{TDX}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\begin{smallmatrix}\text{spanned by}\\
\text{discrete objects}\end{smallmatrix}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\begin{smallmatrix}\text{spanned by}\\
\text{tightly discrete cells}\end{smallmatrix}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}.}

Moreover, 2-cells in the bicategory
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
are
transducer transformations
—natural transformations between profunctors, respecting the grading via reindexing functors (
Definition-Construction 2.13
). The coherence conditions imposed by these 2-cells are studied in detail, we show how they become simple consequence of a double category of which
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
is the bicategory of tightly discrete cells; see
Remark 2.14
,
Remark 2.15
,
Definition 3.1
. The entirety of
section 3
is devoted to the study of universal constructions in the double category
\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}
(limits and colimits, and the existence of companions and conjoints).

We show in
Remark 2.17
that the hom-category
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
admits several equivalent characterizations, given by the multiple ways in which a map of type

(1.14)

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\times(\mathcal{B}^{*})^{\mathrm{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}}

can be curried and uncurried (
item tc1
–
item tc7
of
Remark 2.17
).

The least straightforward of such characterizations is expanded in
Definition 3.12
, finding that
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is a ‘graded coKleisli category’ for a (graded) monad
K_{\mathcal{Q}}=(\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q})
. These perspectives are all useful to concisely derive various (local, i.e. hom-category-wise, and global) properties of
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
; cf.
Corollary 2.18
and (
2.36
) observing that essentially by definition each hom-category
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is cocomplete, and that pre- and post-composition preserve colimits (thus, right liftings and right extensions exist), and that
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
is fibred over
\mathbf{Cat}
, inheriting an enrichment over
\mathbf{Psd}(\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{CAT})
(one can then conjecture that
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
is the truncation of a 3-category; the obstruction in this sense is spelled out in
Remark 2.20
). The characterization as graded coKleisli category of
K_{\mathcal{Q}}
allows to determine that adjoint pairs in
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
are poorly behaved: they can only arise from ordinary adjunctions in
\mathbf{Cat}
, with trivial state categories (
Lemma 3.14
and
Corollary 3.15
).

As it is common for other bicategories where 1-cells are profunctors, hom-categories
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
between finite objects have a rather direct combinatorial interpretation. Let
\mathcal{I}
be the terminal category; in
Example 2.21
we exhibit how the category
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{I},\varnothing)
behaves as a category of pairs (category, endoprofunctor on that category). This is in analogy with linear algebra, where a pair
(V,f)
of a vector space and a linear map over said vector space is just the data of a
k[X]
-module structure on
V
. In
Remark 2.22
, we characterize transducers of type
\varnothing\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.62961pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.03758pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 74.07518pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 74.07518pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{I}
,
\mathcal{I}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.62961pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.03758pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 74.07518pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 74.07518pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{I}
,
\varnothing\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.62961pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.03758pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 74.07518pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 74.07518pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\varnothing
and the way they naturally act on
\mathcal{L}
; this has connections with the category of
\mathbb{N}
-graded sets, and with the construction of free promonads on an endoprofunctor, see (
2.42
).

To add to the compositional structure of
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
, we describe a monoidal structure on the locally posetal bicategory
\mathbf{TDX}
of 1-transducers, turning
\mathbf{TDX}
into a (strict) monoidal 2-category (
subsection 3.2
); such 2-monoidal structure heavily relies on the existence of a tensorial strength for the presheaf construction, cf.
Remark 3.9
. If the composition of 1-cells accounts for the possibility to sequentially stack ‘graded processes’, the monoidal structure accounts for a way to stack them in parallel, compatibly with (i.e. functorially with respect to) the former operation. Something similar happens for other 2-categories of ‘processes’,
[
KSW10
,
KSW97
,
BLLL23a
,
BLLL23b
,
BFL+23
,
LGR+21
]
, which ought to be monoidal bicategories.

The theory of monads in
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
is touched upon in
Definition 3.10
. We show that a monad in
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
, i.e. a functor

(1.15)

\textstyle{t:\mathcal{A}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\times(\mathcal{A}^{*})^{\mathrm{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}}

subject to monad axioms, carries a fairly rich structure: it is a
monoidal promonad
on
\mathcal{A}^{*}
and
graded
over its state category
\mathcal{Q}
, which carries a monoidal structure induced by the multiplication and unit cells (
Theorem 3.11
). The appearance of a grading is a known phenomenon when studying the formal theory of monads for such bicategories of processes; our recent work
[
Lor25
]
outlines that a monad in a pseudo double category where 1-cells are Mealy automata consists of a process where, on top of the alphabet acting on states, states act on the alphabet, and these two actions ‘cohere’ in a suitable sense.

This fact bears an evident resemblance to the fact that (cf.
[
GKO+16
]
) a distributive law between monads
T:M\to\mathbf{Cat},S:N\to\mathbf{Cat}
graded by monoids
M,N
respectively boils down to a matching structure for
M,N
(cf.
[
ABM14
,
ACIM09
,
AM12
,
AM10
]
), and the resulting composite monad
ST
is graded over the ‘bicrossed’ (or ‘Zappa-Szép’) product
M\bowtie N
of
M,N
.

In
subsection 5.1
we show how
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
relates with other bicategories of processes; we show how
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
equips the bicategory
\mathbf{KSW}
of
[
KSW97
]
with proarrows
[
RW88
,
Woo85
,
Woo82
]
, in the sense that there is a locally fully faithful pseudofunctor
\Pi:\mathbf{Cat}\to\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
so that every 1-cell in the image of
\Pi
has a companion and a conjoint in
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
; interestingly,
\Pi
splits as a composition of two locally fully faithful pseudofunctors, neither of which is a proarrow equipment. This is the content of
Remark 4.3
. We also draw a connection with Guitart’s category
\mathbf{MAC}
of machines described in
[
Gui74
]
(but see
[
BLLL23a
, §2]
for a more modern cut on the construction, as well as for the relations between
\mathbf{Mly}
and
\mathbf{MAC}
).

The paper ends with a speculation, in
section 5
, connecting profunctors-as-matrices with the classical theory of differential equations with regular singularities
[
Was65
,
Lev71
,
Boa01
,
Sab07
,
Del70
,
Mal91
]
and differential algebra at large; in simple terms, there are settings where it is common to study systems of differential equations of type

(1.16)

Y^{\prime}(z)=A(z)Y(z),

where
Y(z)
is an
n
-tuple of indeterminate functions, and
A(z)
is a
n\times n
matrix of meromorphic functions (so, series in the field of fractions of formal power series), in the complex variable
z
.

If one is willing to abandon some motivation coming from the theory of state machines, equation (
1.16
) can be categorified as follows: let
\mathcal{Q}=\mathbf{Bij}
be the category of finite sets and bijections (so the free
symmetric
monoidal category
\mathcal{I}^{\sigma}
on a single generator), then one can consider
the category
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{B}}:=\textbf{K}_{\sigma}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
as base of enrichment, and the category of
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{B}}
-enriched combinatorial species
as the functor category with objects
Y:\mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\textbf{K}_{\sigma}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
.

Fix any such functor
Y:\mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\textbf{K}_{\sigma}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
, a profunctor
A:{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\to\textbf{K}_{\sigma}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
, and an equation like

(1.17)

\partial Y\cong A\otimes Y

can be interpreted as an isomorphism between the derivative of
Y
at the LHS of the equation, in the sense of the category of Joyal species,
6
6

6

The derivative of a species is defined as
\partial Y[n]=Y[n+1]
, cf.
[
BLL98
, §1.4]
, cf. also
[
Raj93
]
for a neat elementary introduction and its categorical properties.
and the ‘matrix product’

(1.18)

A\otimes Y=\int^{n:\mathbf{Bij}}A(n,\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,)\otimes Yn:\mathcal{Q}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\textbf{K}_{\sigma}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle

at the RHS, now that
A
can be regarded as a
symmetric 2-transducer

\mathcal{I}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.54623pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.11642pt\raise 4.50694pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.50694pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\sigma}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 78.8668pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 78.8668pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{B}
, i.e. a functor

(1.19)

\textstyle{\mathcal{I}^{\sigma}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{K}_{\sigma}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle,}

strong monoidal in the first argument (because together with an
A
as in (
1.16
), we access all its iterates).

From a distance, this resembles the notion of monoidal distributor
[
GJ17
, 2.1]
, of which we would like to think the theory of 2-transducers as some sort of noncommutative, graded analogue. For the moment, however, we leave this question open for future work.

2.
The bicategory of 2-transducers

We begin by recalling the classical construction of transducers, based on
[
Ber13
, III.6]
and
[
Cho04
, 2.3]
, with adaptations to clarify the categorification process introduced in
Definition-Construction 2.13
. Additional comprehensive references for the algebraic approach to transducers include
[
JB88
,
KS85
,
SS78
]
.

Let
A
be a set (an ‘input alphabet’), and a base semiring
\mathbb{K}
.
7
7

7

Often in the following
\mathbb{K}
is the set of Booleans, equipped with logical XOR and AND operations.
Denote as

(2.1)

A^{*}:=\sum_{n\geq 0}A^{n}=1+A+A\times A+\dots+A^{n}+\dots

the countable coproduct of all
n
-fold products
A^{n}
, for
n\geq 0
. Elements of
A^{*}
are finite ordered lists. The set
A^{*}
is equipped with a monoid structure given by concatenation of lists, denoted as an infix symbol
\mathchoice{\mathop{\scalebox{.75}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.99997pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.55}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.99997pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.45}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.99997pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.25}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.99997pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}
. This gives
A^{*}
the universal property of the
free monoid
on the
carrier set

A
.

The construction
A\mapsto A^{*}
is a functor
\mathbf{Set}\to\mathbf{Mon}
into the category of monoids, left adjoint to the functor
\mathbf{Mon}\to\mathbf{Set}
that forgets the monoid operation.

Definition 2.1

(Monoid algebra of
A
)
.

The
monoid algebra
of
A
(with coefficients in
\mathbb{K}
) is the set, denoted
\langle\!\!\langle A\rangle\!\!\rangle
, of all functions
f:A^{*}\to\mathbb{K}
equipped with pointwise sum in
\mathbb{K}
as addition, and
Cauchy convolution

(2.2)

(f\odot g)(l)=\sum_{p\mathchoice{\mathop{\scalebox{.75}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.63885pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.55}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.63885pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.45}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.63885pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.25}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.63885pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}q=l}f(p)\cdot g(q)

as multiplication. As such,
(\langle\!\!\langle A\rangle\!\!\rangle,+,\odot)
is itself a semiring with additive unit the constant at 0, and multiplicative unit the ‘Dirac delta’ of the empty list,
\delta([])=1
and
\delta(a\mathbin{::}as)=0
.

Note that
\langle\!\!\langle A\rangle\!\!\rangle
has the universal property of the
free noncommutative
\mathbb{K}
-algebra
on the set
A
.

On
\langle\!\!\langle A\rangle\!\!\rangle
there’s also a
restricted star
operation, i.e. a partial function
(\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,)^{\star}:f\mapsto f^{\star}:=\sum_{n\geq 0}f^{\odot n}
, where
f^{\odot n}:=f\odot\dots\odot f
,
n
times,
defined only on
reduced
elements (the
f
’s such that
f\texttt{[\,]}=0
).
8
8

8

The restriction on the domain of
\star
is obviously due to the fact that the star of
f(X)=a_{0}+Xg(X)
is
\Big(\sum_{k\geq 0}a_{0}^{k}\Big)+Xh(X)
, and thus, if
a_{0}\neq 0
, additional conditions of boundedness or a topology on
\mathbb{K}
might be necessary to ensure the existence of
f^{\star}
. Take two examples: the star of
f(X)=\frac{1}{1-X}
(real coefficients) is the Laurent series
\frac{X-1}{X}
, and the star of
f(X)=1
(again real coefficients) doesn’t exist.

Definition 2.2

(Rational series on
A
)
.

The family
\mathrm{Rat}_{\mathbb{K}}(A^{*})
of
rational series
over the semiring
\mathbb{K}
is the
smallest subset
S
of
\langle\!\!\langle A\rangle\!\!\rangle
such that

•

the constant series 0 belongs to
S
;

•

all ‘monomial’ terms
k(a\mathbin{::}\texttt{[\,]})
for
(a,k):A\times\mathbb{K}
are in
S
;

•

if
f,g:S
then
f+g,f\odot g
are in
S
;

•

if
f:S
is reduced, then
f^{\star}:S
.

Definition 2.3
.

If
\textbf{B}=\{0,1\}
is the set of Booleans, given a set
X
,
\mathrm{Rat}_{\textbf{B}}(X)
is the subset of rational Boolean-valued series inside the ring
\textbf{B}\langle\!\!\langle X\rangle\!\!\rangle
.

Remark 2.4
.

Note that
\textbf{B}\langle\!\!\langle X\rangle\!\!\rangle
is naturally identified with the powerset of
X^{*}
, and thus characteristic functions
\chi_{U}:X^{*}\to\textbf{B}
of subsets
U\subseteq X^{*}
are elements of
\textbf{B}\langle\!\!\langle X\rangle\!\!\rangle
in a natural way.

Remark 2.5
.

The set
\textbf{B}\langle\!\!\langle X\rangle\!\!\rangle
exhibits the universal property of the
free quantale
over the set
X
. In fact,
X^{*}
is the free monoidal category over the discrete category
X
, and
\textbf{B}\langle\!\!\langle X\rangle\!\!\rangle
is just the presheaf category over it, cocomplete (since it is a lattice) and symmetric monoidal closed (since it is equipped with pointwise concatenation of subsets, i.e. Day convolution of
B
-enriched presheaves).

Definition 2.6
.

Given two sets
A,B
one defines the
rational relations

R\subseteq A^{*}\times B^{*}
as the smallest subset of
\textbf{B}^{A^{*}\times B^{*}}
containing the singletons and closed under the operations of (countable) subset union, Day convolution, defined for relations
R,S:A^{*}\times B^{*}\to\textbf{B}
as

(2.3)

(R\odot S)(\underline{x},\underline{y})=\bigvee_{\begin{subarray}{c}\underline{a}\mathchoice{\mathop{\scalebox{.75}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.63885pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.55}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.63885pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.45}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.63885pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.25}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.63885pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}\underline{a}^{\prime}=\underline{x}\\
\underline{b}\mathchoice{\mathop{\scalebox{.75}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.63885pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.55}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.63885pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.45}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.63885pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.25}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.63885pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}\underline{b}^{\prime}=\underline{y}\end{subarray}}(\underline{a}\,R\,\underline{b})\land(\underline{a}^{\prime}\,S\,\underline{b}^{\prime})

and restricted star, defined as
R^{\star}:=\bigvee_{n\geq 0}R^{\odot n}
; the subset
R\subseteq A^{*}\times B^{*}
is a rational relation if and only if for every
u:A^{*}
the series
R_{u}:B^{*}\to\textbf{B}
defined as
v\mapsto[\text{is }(u,v)\in R?]
is rational as an element of
\textbf{B}\langle\!\!\langle B\rangle\!\!\rangle
in the sense of
Definition 2.2
.

Definition 2.7

(1-transducer)
.

A (finite)
transducer
consists of a (finite) set
Q
and a monoid homomorphism
A^{*}\to M(Q,\mathrm{Rat}(B))
into the (semi)ring of
|Q|\times|Q|
matrices with coefficients in
\mathrm{Rat}(B)
.

So far, God’s in his heaven, all is right with the world. Now, let’s take all this up a notch, and consider a complete, cocomplete, symmetric monoidal closed base of enrichment
\mathcal{V}
.
9
9

9

In specific examples, the base
\mathcal{V}
will be the category of sets, or the category of Booleans –regarded as the full subcategory of the former spanned by the objects
\{\varnothing,\top\}
. The constructions we outline are valid whenever
\mathcal{V}
is a Bénabou cosmos, such that
both
tabulators and cotabulators in the double category of
\mathcal{V}
-profunctors.
Given a
\mathcal{V}
-category
\mathcal{B}
, the
\mathcal{V}
-category
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat}[{(\mathcal{B}^{*})}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{V}]
is the usual one defined e.g. in
[
Kel05
]
, having objects the
\mathcal{V}
-presheaves
(\mathcal{B}^{*})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathcal{V}
.

2.1.
Two-dimensional transducers

It is straightforward to give the following definition of a monoidal category
\mathbf{Rat}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{B})
, as a categorification of
\mathrm{Rat}(B)
in
Definition 2.8
. We record the definition for completeness, but we will not make much use of it, since there exist bases of enrichment that are far more natural from the category-theoretic standpoint.

Definition 2.8

(Rational presheaf)
.

Let
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
be two
\mathcal{V}
-enriched categories; define
\mathbf{Rat}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{B})
as the smallest full subcategory of
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat}[{\mathcal{B}^{*}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{V}]
such that

rp
1)

the initial object
\varnothing
of
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat}[{\mathcal{B}^{*}}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{V}]
is in
\mathbf{Rat}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{B}^{*})
;

rp
2)

all representables are in
\mathbf{Rat}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{B}^{*})
;

rp
3)

if
F,G:\mathbf{Rat}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{B}^{*})
, then the coproduct
F+G
and the Day convolution
F\odot G
are in
\mathbf{Rat}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{B}^{*})
;

rp
4)

if
F:\mathbf{Rat}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{B}^{*})
is reduced (i.e. if
F(\texttt{[\,]})=\varnothing
), then
F^{\star}
, defined as
\sum_{n\geq 0}F^{\odot n}
, is also in
\mathbf{Rat}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{B}^{*})
.

Note that, as a consequence of
rp3
,
\mathbf{Rat}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{B}^{*})
consists of the closure under restricted star of the finite coproduct completion of
\mathcal{B}^{*}
. Such finite coproduct completion has the universal property of the
free
(cocartesian)
2-rig
on
\mathcal{B}
, in the sense of
[
LT23
]
. So, there is a certain universal property that
\mathbf{Rat}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{B}^{*})
satisfies.

From this, one could define a
2-transducer
as a strong monoidal functor

(2.4)

\textstyle{t:\mathcal{A}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Rat}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{B}^{*})\text{-}\mathbf{Prof}(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{Q}).}

It is however difficult to work with enriched categories and profunctors over
\mathbf{Rat}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{B}^{*})
, as this base of enrichment is not cocomplete (the free cocompletion of a category
\mathcal{C}
under a class of colimits
\Psi
can in principle acquire more colimits, but just those in the
closure
of
\Psi
under iterating the formal addition of
\Psi
-shaped colimits; see
[
AK88
]
). Although frameworks for addressing this issue exist, treating
\mathbf{Rat}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{B}^{*})
-enriched profunctors as a
virtual double category
, they would lead us astray from this first step in understanding categorified transducers. So, motivated by the fact that in
Definition 2.7
above we have considered a posetal base of enrichment, where the free coproduct- and the free colimit-completion coincide, we instead refer to the more general, and more well-behaved, category of profunctors enriched over the free completion
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat}[{(\mathcal{B}^{*})}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{V}]
of
\mathcal{B}
. The category obtained from
\mathcal{B}
first taking the free monoidal
\mathcal{V}
-category
\mathcal{B}^{*}
on
\mathcal{B}
, and considering the closure under coproducts of the image of the Yoneda embedding enjoys the universal property of the
free
(cocomplete)
2-rig
on
\mathcal{B}
, in the sense of
[
LT23
]
. This satisfies a stronger (better, for our purposes) universal property than
\mathbf{Rat}_{\mathcal{V}}(\mathcal{B}^{*})
.

There is a vast literature on categories enriched over
\mathcal{V}
-presheaves, dating back to R. Wood’s PhD thesis
[
Woo78
,
Woo76
]
(written under the supervision of Paré) and recently further studied and adapted in
[
LW25
,
MU22a
]
. Applications of such categories to automata theory are not new, but only appear in a somewhat cryptic remark in
[
Gui80
, 2.1.5°]
citing Wood’s thesis as a reference for what is there called a ‘bicatégorie graduée’.

In the following, for the sake of concise notation, we write
\textbf{K}_{\mathcal{V}}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
as shorthand for the category
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat}[{(\mathcal{B}^{*})}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{V}]
, and
\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
when
\mathcal{V}=\mathbf{Set}
, an assumption we make now until further notice. This is meant to evoke the idea that
\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
contains ‘polynomials’ in variables determined by the category
\mathcal{B}
.

This long series of preliminary observations finally paves the way to the following

Definition 2.9

(2-transducer)
.

A
2-transducer

t:\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 33.20729pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 38.50975pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 83.01952pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 83.01952pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{B}
consists of a pair
(\mathcal{Q},t)
where
\mathcal{Q}
is a category and
t
is a strong monoidal functor

(2.5)

\textstyle{t:\mathcal{A}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\text{-}\mathbf{Prof}(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{Q}),}

where
\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\text{-}\mathbf{Prof}(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{Q})
has objects the functors
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\to\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
(or, which is equivalent,
\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
-enriched functors with domain the free
\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
-enriched category over
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}
), and
\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
-enriched natural transformations, and monoidal structure given by composition of 1-cells.

Giving evidence that this definition makes sense is a process composed of two parts:

•

the definition ‘unwinds well’ and actually reduces to the old one when instantiated in the more canonical setting; ultimately, this follows from
Remark 2.5
.

•

The definition sheds a better light on the decategorified topic.

Establishing this point is the purpose of this note. To do so, let’s unpack the type of
t
in (
2.5
): it uncurries to a functor
\textstyle{t:\mathcal{A}^{*}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle}

such that there are structural isomorphisms

t(l\mathbin{\raisebox{2.0pt}{$\mathchoice{\mathop{\scalebox{.75}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.99997pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.55}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.99997pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.45}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.99997pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.25}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.99997pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}$}}l^{\prime},\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,,\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,)\cong t(l,\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,,\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,)\diamond t(l^{\prime},\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,,\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,)\quad\text{and}\quad t(\texttt{[\,]},\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,,\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,)\cong\hom_{\mathcal{Q}}(\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,,\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,)

compatibly with the bicategory structure (associators and unitors). In turn, such a
t
further uncurries into a functor
\textstyle{t:\mathcal{A}^{*}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\times(\mathcal{B}^{*})^{\mathrm{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}}

qualifying each
t_{a}
as a ‘
\mathcal{Q}
-by-
\mathcal{Q}
matrix of
\mathcal{B}
-valued power series’.

Given that the domain category of a 2-transducer
t
is free monoidal,
t
is uniquely determined by its restriction to
\mathcal{A}
, i.e. on ‘generators’
(a\mathbin{::}\texttt{[\,]})
of
\mathcal{A}^{*}
, so that in fact
t
is completely determined by a functor
t_{0}
of type
\mathcal{A}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\to\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
. In the following we will blur the notational distinction
t
and
t_{0}
as this is rarely a source of confusion, but it is worth to stress again the obvious fact that a transducer is
either
a mere functor with domain
\mathcal{A}
,
or
a strong monoidal one with domain
\mathcal{A}^{*}
.

Remark 2.10

(Structure constants of a 2-transducer)
.

We term the profunctors
t_{a}=t_{0}(a\mathbin{::}\texttt{[\,]}):\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\text{-}\mathbf{Prof}(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{Q})
the
structure constants
of the 2-transducer, in analogy with representation theory
[
Hum72
,
Ser92
]
: given a finite-dimensional Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
(with a basis
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}
for its underlying vector spaces), the scalars
{\gamma_{ab}}^{c}
appear as coordinates of the Lie bracket of two basis element
[e_{a},e_{b}]=\sum_{c}{\gamma_{ab}}^{c}\,e_{c}
.

Remark 2.11
.

It is worth to spell out in detail what strong monoidality means for a 2-transducer; for any list of objects
\underline{a}=(a_{1},\dots,a_{n}):\mathcal{A}^{*}
, the functor
t
is determined on the generators in the sense that

(2.6)

t(\underline{a},\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,):(q,q^{\prime},\underline{b})\mapsto\int^{x_{1},\dots,x_{n-1}}t_{a_{1}}(q,x_{1})(\underline{b})\times t_{a_{2}}(x_{1},x_{2})(\underline{b})\times\dots\times t_{a_{n}}(x_{n-1},q^{\prime})(\underline{b})

Remark 2.12

(Two-step extension of a 2-transducer)
.

The
Yoneda extension
of a functor
F:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
with small domain and cocomplete codomain is the left Kan extension of
F
along the Yoneda embedding of
\mathcal{X}
; when
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{A}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}
and the codomain is of the form
\mathcal{Y}=\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
, one can perform a ‘two-step extension’ of a transducer
t:\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.38234pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 37.47855pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 80.95709pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 80.95709pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{B}
, first extending
t
to a functor
\mathcal{A}^{*}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\to\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
monoidal in the
\mathcal{A}
component, and then taking the Yoneda extension ‘in the variable
\mathcal{A}
’ (i.e. leaving the
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}
components alone), to the effect that each 2-transducer induces a functor

(2.7)

\textstyle{T:\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{A}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle}

obtained as
\mathrm{Lan}_{\boldsymbol{y}_{\mathcal{A}^{*}}}t
, which is monoidally cocontinuous in the
\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{A}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
component. One could term this two-step operation sending
t
to
T=\text{LY}(T)
the
Laurent-Yoneda extension
of
t
.

The Laurent-Yoneda extension
t\mapsto\text{LT}(t)
should be thought of as the Kleisli extension for a certain monad (each extension of
t
is the Kleisli extension with respect to a pseudomonad); perhaps this observation can be useful from a formal viewpoint, but we will not expand on it in the present work.

Definition-Construction 2.13

(The bicategory
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
)
.

Given all this, for fixed
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
, there exists a category
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
having

t
1)

objects the pairs
(\mathcal{Q},t)
as in (
2.5
); we term the category
\mathcal{Q}
the ‘state category’ of the 2-transducer;

t
2)

morphisms
(\mathcal{P},t)\to(\mathcal{Q},t^{\prime})
the ‘transducer transformations’ i.e. the pairs
(F,\theta)
where
F:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
is a functor, and
\theta:t\Rightarrow t^{\prime}(F,F)
, is a natural transformation, where
t^{\prime}(F,F)
is defined as the composition

(2.8)

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{P}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}\times{F}^{\mathrm{op}}\times F}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle.}

Letting
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
vary,
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,,\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,)
is a bifunctor
\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{Cat}\to\mathbf{Cat}
;
10
10

10

Into large categories, but we will not pay much attention to this detail, and in general to size issues.
any two functors

(2.9)

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G}
\textstyle{\mathcal{D}}

will induce a functor
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(F,G):\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\to\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{D})
defined sending
(\mathcal{Q},t):\mathcal{A}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\to\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle

(2.10)

\textstyle{\mathcal{C}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

and where

sending it to the presheaf

\underline{d}\mapsto\int^{\underline{b}:\mathcal{B}^{*}}P\underline{b}\times\mathcal{D}^{*}(\underline{d},G^{*}\underline{b})

where

is the obvious extension of

to a functor between monoidal categories. All in all, for every

is defined as

(\underline{c},q,q^{\prime})\mapsto\int^{\underline{b}:\mathcal{B}^{*}}t(F\underline{c},q,q^{\prime})(\underline{b})\times\mathcal{D}^{*}(\underline{d},G^{*}\underline{b}).

Moreover,

•

given categories
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C}
we can define bifunctors

\textstyle{\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,\circ\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,:\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{B},\mathcal{C})\times\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

obeying the axioms of a (horizontal) composition law; this is defined, given 2-transducers
(s,\mathcal{Q}):\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 48.73889pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 57.92455pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 121.8491pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 121.8491pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{B}
and
(t,\mathcal{P}):\mathcal{B}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 48.73889pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 57.92455pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 121.8491pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 121.8491pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{C}
, as the 2-transducer
(\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q},T\circ(s\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{P}))
obtained from the composition

(2.14)

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{*}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{P}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{P}}
\textstyle{\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{P}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{C}}\rangle\!\!\rangle}

where
T:=\mathrm{Lan}_{\boldsymbol{y}_{\mathcal{B}}}t
is the two-step extension of
t
in the
\mathcal{B}
component, as in
Remark 2.12
. We can then write the composition
t\circ s:\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.87537pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.84499pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 99.68997pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 99.68997pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{C}
in coend language as

\displaystyle\lambda\underline{c}.\Big((t\circ s)(\underline{a};(p,q),(p^{\prime},q^{\prime}))[\underline{c}]\Big)

\displaystyle=\lambda\underline{c}.\Big(T(s(\underline{a},q,q^{\prime})[\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,],p,p^{\prime})[\underline{c}]\Big)

\displaystyle=\lambda\underline{c}.\Big(\int^{\underline{b}:{\mathcal{B}}^{*}}s(\underline{a},q,q^{\prime})[\underline{b}]\times t(\underline{b};p,p^{\prime})[\underline{c}]\Big).

In each
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A})
there is a distinguished element, the
identity
2-transducer
\iota:\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.87537pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 46.84499pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 99.68997pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 99.68997pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{A}
with state category
\mathcal{I}
the terminal category (discrete on a singleton set), and structure constants
\iota_{a}
the representable functor at the singleton list
(a\mathbin{::}\texttt{[\,]})
. More precisely,

(2.15)

\textstyle{\iota:\mathcal{A}^{*}\times\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{A}}\rangle\!\!\rangle}

is the Yoneda embedding up to the isomorphism
\mathcal{A}^{*}\times\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{I}\cong\mathcal{A}^{*}
.

This gives rise to a bicategory
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
of (small) categories, 2-transducers
t:\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 22.2223pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.7783pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 55.55663pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 55.55663pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{B}

\displaystyle((u\circ v)\circ w)(\underline{a};(p,q,r),(p^{\prime},q^{\prime},r^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\cong\int^{\underline{b}:\mathcal{B}^{*}}\kern-7.5pt\int^{\underline{c}:\mathcal{C}^{*}}w(\underline{a},p,p^{\prime})[\underline{b}]\times\Big(v(\underline{b};q,q^{\prime})[\underline{c}]\times u(\underline{c},r,r^{\prime})\Big)

\displaystyle(u\circ(v\circ w))(\underline{a};(p,q,r),(p^{\prime},q^{\prime},r^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\cong\int^{\underline{c}:{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}(v\circ w)(\underline{a},(p,q),(p,q)^{\prime})[\underline{c}]\times u(\underline{c};r,r^{\prime})

\displaystyle\cong\int^{\underline{c}:\mathcal{C}^{*}}\left(\int^{\underline{b}:{\mathcal{B}}^{*}}w(\underline{a},p,p^{\prime})[\underline{b}]\times v(\underline{b};q,q^{\prime})\right)\times u(\underline{c},r,r^{\prime})

\displaystyle\cong\int^{\underline{c}:\mathcal{C}^{*}}\kern-7.5pt\int^{\underline{b}:\mathcal{B}^{*}}\Big(w(\underline{a},p,p^{\prime})[\underline{b}]\times v(\underline{b};q,q^{\prime})[\underline{c}]\Big)\times u(\underline{c},r,r^{\prime})
