\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 22.2223pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 15.94147pt\raise 6.5pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{(\mathcal{Q},t)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 55.55663pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 55.55663pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{B}
are induced by the isomorphisms

\displaystyle(\iota_{\mathcal{B}}\circ t)(\underline{a};(*,q),(*,q)^{\prime})

\displaystyle\cong\int^{\underline{b}:\mathcal{B}^{*}}t(\underline{a};q,q^{\prime})[\underline{b}]\times\iota(\underline{b};*,*)

\displaystyle\cong t(\underline{a};q,q^{\prime}).

\displaystyle(t\circ\iota_{\mathcal{A}})(\underline{a};(q,*),(q,*)^{\prime})

\displaystyle\cong\int^{\underline{a}^{\prime}:\mathcal{A}^{*}}\iota_{\mathcal{A}}(\underline{a}^{\prime},*,*)[\underline{a}]\times t(a^{\prime};q,q^{\prime})

\displaystyle\cong\int^{\underline{a}^{\prime}:\mathcal{A}^{*}}\mathcal{A}^{*}(\underline{a}^{\prime},\underline{a})\times t(a^{\prime};q,q^{\prime})

\displaystyle\cong t(\underline{a};q,q^{\prime}).

Rote computation yields all the needed coherences (pentagon, unit axioms) in order to define the bicategory structure on
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
.

Every 2-cell

induces, by functoriality and by the universal properties of the relevant coends, a natural transformation

\textstyle{\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{A}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{P}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

(2.22)

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{P}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

where
K
is the transpose of

It is straightforward to verify that left and right whiskering are compatible, i.e.

(\mathcal{K},k)*\big((F,\theta)*(\mathcal{X},h)\big)=\big((\mathcal{K},k)*(F,\theta)\big)*(\mathcal{X},h).

Remark 2.16
.

There is a natural way to regard a profunctor
p:\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 23.72835pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 26.6609pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 59.32181pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 59.32181pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{B}
as a 2-transducer
(\mathcal{I},\bar{p}):\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 23.72835pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 26.6609pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 59.32181pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 59.32181pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{B}
, where
\mathcal{I}
is the terminal category;
\bar{p}
is the unique profunctor making the triangle

(2.24)

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\times{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{I}\times(\mathcal{B}^{*})^{\mathrm{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\bar{p}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}

commutative, and constant at the empty set
\varnothing
for every other summand

of
\mathcal{B}^{*}

.
This straightforward construction provides a natural embedding
\mathbf{Prof}\subseteq\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
(or an embedding of double categories
\mathbb{P}\mathbf{rof}\subseteq\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}
). It is immediate to check that the embedding is compatible with the ‘Laurent-Yoneda’ extension of

.

Remark 2.17

(Many versions of a category)
.

We can describe the category
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
in many different ways; each of these will prove useful in different contexts, which we mention while we outline the equivalence. All the following categories are equivalent.

tc
1)

The category of functors of type
\mathcal{A}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\to\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
, and the category of functors
\mathcal{A}^{*}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\to\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
, strong monoidal in the variable
\mathcal{A}
, given the universal property of
\mathcal{A}^{*}
(by currying,
\mathcal{A}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\to\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
corresponds to
\mathcal{A}\to\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\text{-}\mathbf{Prof}(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{Q})
, and the codomain of the latter is monoidal under composition).

tc
2)

The category of functors
\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{A}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\to\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
, taking the two-step extension in the
\mathcal{A}
component, and (by currying) the category of functors
t:{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\to\mathbf{Cat}(\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{A}}\rangle\!\!\rangle,\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle)
, so that each
t_{qq^{\prime}}:\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{A}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\to\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
is a left adjoint, by a well-known property of profunctors;

tc
3)

currying in another direction,
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is the category of profunctors from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{B}
, enriched over the (monoidal) category
\mathbf{Set}^{{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}}
of endoprofunctors on
\mathcal{Q}
.

tc
4)

Currying in yet another direction,
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
can be seen as the category
\mathbf{Cat}({\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q},\mathbf{Set}^{\mathcal{A}^{*}\times(\mathcal{B}^{*})^{\mathrm{op}}})=\mathbf{Set}^{\mathcal{A}^{*}\times(\mathcal{B}^{*})^{\mathrm{op}}}\text{-}\mathbf{Prof}(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{Q})
, where the codomain
\mathbf{Set}^{\mathcal{A}^{*}\times(\mathcal{B}^{*})^{\mathrm{op}}}
is monoidal when equipped with Day convolution (
\mathcal{A}^{*}\times(\mathcal{B}^{*})^{\mathrm{op}}
is monoidal in the ‘obvious’ way,
(\underline{a},\underline{b})\otimes(\underline{a}^{\prime},\underline{b}^{\prime}):=(\underline{a}\mathchoice{\mathop{\scalebox{.75}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.99997pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.55}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.99997pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.45}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.99997pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.25}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.99997pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}\underline{a}^{\prime},\underline{b}\mathbin{\mathchoice{\mathop{\scalebox{.75}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.99997pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.55}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.99997pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.45}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.99997pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}{\mathop{\scalebox{.25}{$\boldsymbol{+}\kern-1.99997pt\boldsymbol{+}$}}}^{\mathrm{op}}}\underline{b}^{\prime})
).

tc
5)

\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is also equivalent to the category
\mathbf{Psd}(\Sigma\mathcal{A}^{*},\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\text{-}\mathbf{Prof})
of pseudofunctors

(2.25)

\textstyle{\boldsymbol{t}:\Sigma\mathcal{A}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\text{-}\mathbf{Prof}}

where
\Sigma\mathcal{A}^{*}
is the one-object bicategory associated to
\mathcal{A}^{*}
; the unique object is mapped by

to
\mathcal{Q}

tc

\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})

\mathcal{A}^{*}\times\mathcal{Q}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 25.06993pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@stopper}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 28.3379pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 62.67581pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 62.67581pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{B}^{*}

Corollary 2.18
.

Some of the equivalent characterizations above make it easier to establish certain properties of
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
, they have interesting corollaries or suggestive interpretations: we collect a few here and in
Example 2.21
,
Remark 2.20
below. Some easy consequences are that
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
admits all small colimits, by virtue of
tc3
. Furthermore, from
tc5
it follows that, at least informally and over bases of enrichment where a Grothendieck construction for presheaves is available,
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
can be thought of as the category of
Conduché functors
over categories of the form
\Sigma\mathcal{A}^{*}
, where fibres are
\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
-enriched.
11
11

11

Admittedly, this is a bit imprecise, but the appearance of a Conduché property doesn’t seem random here. A connection between automata theory and the Conduché property has been observed, in passing and using a different terminology, in
[
MZ25
, §2.3]
, and before that it has been employed in
[
KL10
]
to study concurrency and bisimulation, linking process semantics to functorial factorisation properties; the latter reference is in particular focused with studying a Conduché property for functors between categories enriched in a locally posetal 2-category obtained from a free monoid
A^{*}
with respect to the prefix order.

Our choice to focus on
\mathbf{Set}
as base of enrichment is motivated by the fact that here we acquire this additional point of view.

It has just been observed that each hom-category
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
admits all small colimits; furthermore, both pre- and post-composition are easily seen to be colimit-preserving; one can then suspect that each functor
\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,\circ s
and
t\circ\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,
has a right adjoint (respectively, the right extension along
s
, and the right lifting along
t
). We now construct right extensions and lifts explicitly, mimicking what happens in the bicategory of profunctors.

Remark 2.19

(Construction of right extensions and lifts in
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
)
.

Consider two composable 2-transducers
(s,\mathcal{Q}):\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.97502pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 40.71945pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 87.43892pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 87.43892pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{B}
and
(t,\mathcal{P}):\mathcal{B}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.97502pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 40.71945pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 87.43892pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 87.43892pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{C}
as in (
2.14
) above; there is an evident bijective correspondence between 2-cells
(F,\theta)
of type

and 2-cells

of type

,

(2.30)

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{*}\times{(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{P})}^{\mathrm{op}}\times(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{P})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t\circ s}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\theta}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}^{*}\times{F}^{\mathrm{op}}\times F}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{*}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}^{*}\times{\hat{F}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\hat{F}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\hat{\theta}}
\textstyle{\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{C}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{*}\times{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{N}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

constructed as follows. Given a 2-cell

is evidently the mate

is determined under the chain of bijections

\displaystyle\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{C}}\rangle\!\!\rangle((t\circ s)(\underline{a},pqp^{\prime}q^{\prime}),r(\underline{a},Fpq,Fp^{\prime}q^{\prime}))

\displaystyle\cong\int_{\underline{c}:\mathcal{C}^{*}}\mathbf{Set}\Big(\int^{\underline{b}:{\mathcal{B}}^{*}}s(\underline{a},q,q^{\prime})[\underline{b}]\times t(\underline{b};p,p^{\prime})[\underline{c}],r(\underline{a},Fpq,Fp^{\prime}q^{\prime})[\underline{c}]\Big)

\displaystyle\cong\int_{\underline{c}:\mathcal{C}^{*}}\int_{\underline{b}:\mathcal{B}^{*}}\mathbf{Set}\big(s(\underline{a},q,q^{\prime})[\underline{b}]\times t(\underline{b};p,p^{\prime})[\underline{c}],r(\underline{a},Fpq,Fp^{\prime}q^{\prime})[\underline{c}]\big)

\displaystyle\cong\int_{\underline{c}:\mathcal{C}^{*}}\int_{\underline{b}:\mathcal{B}^{*}}\mathbf{Set}\big(s(\underline{a},q,q^{\prime})[\underline{b}],\big\{t(\underline{b};p,p^{\prime})[\underline{c}],r(\underline{a},Fpq,Fp^{\prime}q^{\prime})[\underline{c}]\big\}\big)

\displaystyle\cong\int_{\underline{b}:\mathcal{B}^{*}}\mathbf{Set}\Big(s(\underline{a},q,q^{\prime})[\underline{b}],\int_{\underline{c}:\mathcal{C}^{*}}\big\{t(\underline{b};p,p^{\prime})[\underline{c}],r(\underline{a},Fpq,Fp^{\prime}q^{\prime})[\underline{c}]\big\}\Big)

which is exactly a natural transformation

(2.31)

\textstyle{s(\underline{a},q,q^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\langle t/r\rangle(\underline{a},\hat{F}q,\hat{F}q^{\prime})}

if
\hat{F}q=F(-,q)
and we define
\langle t/r\rangle:\mathcal{A}^{*}\times{(\mathcal{N}^{\mathcal{P}})}^{\mathrm{op}}\times(\mathcal{N}^{\mathcal{P}})\to\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle
as sending
(\underline{a},W,W^{\prime}):\mathcal{A}^{*}\times{(\mathcal{N}^{\mathcal{P}})}^{\mathrm{op}}\times(\mathcal{N}^{\mathcal{P}})
to the presheaf on
\mathcal{B}^{*}
given by the end

(2.32)

\underline{b}\mapsto\int_{\underline{c}:\mathcal{C}^{*}}\big\{t(\underline{b};p,p^{\prime})[\underline{c}],r(\underline{a},Wp,Wp^{\prime})[\underline{c}]\big\}.

Reasoning similarly, one determines a bijective correspondence between cells of the two types

(2.33)

\textstyle{\star\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t\circ s}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\theta}
\textstyle{\star\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\tilde{\theta}}
\textstyle{\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{C}}\rangle\!\!\rangle}
\textstyle{\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{C}}\rangle\!\!\rangle}
\textstyle{\star\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r}
\textstyle{\star\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\langle r/s\rangle}

where the right extension
\langle r/s\rangle
of
r
along
s
is defined as the end

(2.34)

\int_{\underline{b}:\mathcal{B}^{*}}\big\{s(\underline{a},q,q^{\prime})[\underline{b}],r(\underline{a},Uq,Uq^{\prime})[\underline{c}]\big\}.

We end this round of consequences of the many equivalent characterizations in
Remark 2.17
with a somewhat technical observation.

Remark 2.20
.

From
tc2
above it follows that every hom-category of
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
is the total category of a fibration over
\mathbf{Cat}
, because the assignment
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}=\mathbf{Prof}_{\mathbf{Set}^{\mathcal{A}^{*}\times(\mathcal{B}^{*})^{\mathrm{op}}}}
sending
\mathcal{Q}
to the category of endoprofunctors on
\mathcal{Q}
, enriched over
\mathbf{Set}^{\mathcal{A}^{*}\times(\mathcal{B}^{*})^{\mathrm{op}}}
is functorial (and contravariant).

So, we can think of
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
as the Grothendieck-Bénabou construction of
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}}
.

As a consequence,
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is small complete and cocomplete, and the assignment

(2.35)

\textstyle{(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\int_{\mathcal{Q}}\Big(\mathbf{Prof}_{\mathbf{Set}^{\mathcal{A}^{*}\times(\mathcal{B}^{*})^{\mathrm{op}}}}\Big)\cong\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})}

qua
(pseudo)promonad, factors through the domain functor
dom
yielding the total category of a fibration, i.e.

(2.36)

\textstyle{\mathbf{FIB}/\mathbf{Cat}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
dom
\textstyle{\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathbf{Cat}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(-,-)}
\textstyle{\mathbf{CAT}}

This means that every hom-category
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is a pseudofunctor
\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{CAT}
, i.e. that
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
is a
\mathbf{Psd}(\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathbf{CAT})
-enriched 2-category
, or (for lack of a better name) a ‘graded 2-category’. Treating it as a 3-category –after all,
\mathbf{FIB}/\mathbf{Cat}
has 2-cells!– is however not completely straightforward:
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
is not a 2-category (and
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
not a 3-category), because the functor
\mathcal{Q}\mapsto{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}
is not a 2-functor (what would be its covariance type on 2-cells?).

Instead, let’s focus on a series of parallels with linear algebra, inspired by the discussion so far.

Example 2.21

(‘Linear algebra’ in
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
)
.

Let
k
be a field. A
k
-vector space
V
equipped with an endomorphism
f:V\to V
corresponds to a
k[X]
-module structure on
V
(by the universal property of
k[X]
as the free
k
-algebra on one generator); here we would like to argue that such a notion is categorified by 2-transducers
\mathcal{I}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.91164pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 21.88994pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 49.77988pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 49.77988pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\varnothing
so that, if by ‘linear map between vector spaces’ we choose to mean ‘colimit-preserving functor between cocomplete categories’,
\mathcal{L}=\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{I},\varnothing)
can be thought of as a category of categories equipped with an endoprofunctor.

Indeed
Definition 2.9
yields that a 2-transducer
\mathcal{I}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.91164pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 21.88994pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 49.77988pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 49.77988pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\varnothing
consists of a pair
(\mathcal{Q},t)
where
\mathcal{Q}
is a category and
t
a functor of type

(2.37)

\textstyle{\mathcal{I}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\varnothing}\rangle\!\!\rangle}

which (using the fact that
\mathcal{I}^{*}=\mathbb{N},\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\varnothing}\rangle\!\!\rangle=\mathbf{Set}
) corresponds to a single profunctor
t=t_{*}:{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\to\mathbf{Set}
, together with all its iterates
t\circ\dots\circ t
. Now
tc2
yields that a 2-transducer
\mathcal{I}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 22.53392pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 25.16783pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 56.33568pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 56.33568pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\varnothing
also extends uniquely to a unique functor

(2.38)

\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}/\mathbb{N}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}}

which is a
(q,q^{\prime})
-wise a left adjoint (the presheaf category of
\mathbb{N}
regarded as discrete category is the slice
\mathbf{Set}/\mathbb{N}
): so
T
is in this case just given by the ‘polynomial of endofunctors’ sending
((S_{n}\mid n:\mathbb{N}),q,q^{\prime}):\mathbf{Set}/\mathbb{N}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}
to

(2.39)

T((S_{n}),q,q^{\prime})=\sum_{n:\mathbb{N}}S_{n}\times t^{n}(q,q^{\prime}).

Compare this expression with the representation of how a linear endomorphism
T:V\to V
of a
k
-vector space
V
yields a
k[X]
representation acting with a polynomial
g(X)=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}X^{i}
on a vector
v:V
as
\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}T^{i}(v)
, cf.
[
Gri07
, VIII.7.1]
.

By a similar token, more generally, the category
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\varnothing)
can be thought of as a category of pairs
(\mathcal{Q},\{t_{a}\})
where
t:\mathcal{A}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\to\mathbf{Set}
is an
\mathcal{A}
-indexed profunctor. This somehow categorifies the well-known notion of a (
A
-labeled, nondeterministic) transition system, usually presented as a function
A\times Q\to\textbf{B}^{Q}
(or more conveniently, as a coalgebra for the functor
\textbf{B}^{\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,\times A}
). The previous discussion substantiates the idea that hom-categories
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
have a rather direct combinatorial interpretation when
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
are ‘very small’.

Remark 2.22

(2-transducers between categories of very low cardinality)
.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be the terminal category, and
\mathcal{M}=\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I})
the hom-category of transducers
\mathcal{I}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 26.1838pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.73026pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 65.46053pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 65.46053pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{I}
; clearly,

is monoidal with respect to composition, and
\mathcal{L}
above is a
\mathcal{M}
-bimodule. Equally clearly, it is also an
\mathcal{N}=\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\varnothing,\varnothing)
-left module, but
\mathcal{N}
has way less structure and its action is quite more trivial.
12
12

12

In more detail:
\mathcal{N}
has objects the pairs
(\mathcal{Q},\hom_{\mathcal{Q}})
of a category plus its identity profunctor, and morphisms all the functors
F:\mathcal{P}\to\mathcal{Q}
; as such, it is monoidally equivalent to
\mathcal{V}\text{-}\mathbf{Cat}
. The composition as defined in
Equation 2.14
is easily seen to be isomorphic to the projection
\mathcal{N}\times\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{L}
.

The category
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I})
is easily seen to consist of pairs
(\mathcal{Q},s:{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\to\mathbf{Set}/\mathbb{N})
, and thus the composition
\mathcal{I}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 20.51668pt\raise 6.5pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 65.46053pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{I}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 26.1838pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\varnothing
as outlined in
Equation 2.14
boils down to the functor

(2.40)

\textstyle{{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{P}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{P}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}/\mathbb{N}\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{P}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}}

sending
(q,q^{\prime},p,p^{\prime})
to
\sum_{n:\mathbb{N}}s(q,q^{\prime})_{n}\times t^{n}(p,p^{\prime})
.
It is also interesting to work out what the composition map

(2.41)

\textstyle{\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{I},\varnothing)\times\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\varnothing,\mathcal{I})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\varnothing,\varnothing)}

boils down to given these characterizations; the result must reduce to the hom-functor of a category, but which one? Consider a diagram of shape

(2.42)

\textstyle{\varnothing\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathcal{Q},s)}
\textstyle{\mathcal{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathcal{P},t)}
\textstyle{\varnothing}

the 2-transducer
(\mathcal{Q},s)
is just the hom-functor of a category
\mathcal{Q}
, enriched over
\mathbf{Set}/\mathbb{N}
in the trivial way, i.e. describing the
\mathbb{N}
-graded set constant at
\mathcal{Q}(q,q^{\prime})
. So, the composition in (
2.40
) reduces to

\displaystyle T((S_{n}),q,q^{\prime})

\displaystyle\cong\sum_{n:\mathbb{N}}s(q,q^{\prime})_{n}\times t^{n}(p,p^{\prime})

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{Q}(q,q^{\prime})\times\sum_{n:\mathbb{N}}t^{n}(p,p^{\prime})

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{Q}(q,q^{\prime})\times\mathcal{P}_{t^{*}}(p,p^{\prime})=(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{P}_{t^{*}})\big((q,p),(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})\big)

where
\mathcal{P}_{t^{*}}
is the category obtained from the free promonad
\sum_{n}t^{n}
on
\mathcal{P}
, and
\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{P}_{t^{*}}
the product of categories.
13
13

13

Last, one can consider a composition of cells of the same type, but in the opposite direction,

\textstyle{\mathcal{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathcal{P},t)}
\textstyle{\varnothing\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathcal{Q},s)}
\textstyle{\mathcal{I}}

, yielding a functor of type
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\to\mathbf{Set}/\mathbb{N}
defined as the trivial enrichment of
t(p,p^{\prime})
over
\mathbf{Set}/\mathbb{N}
.

Remark 2.23
.

What interpretation does the above construction have? Among different analogies one can find for (
2.40
) in the realm of linear algebra, the following seems the most streamlined: let
k
be a field; let
\mathbb{N}\pitchfork k\cong\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}k
be the
power
of
k
by
\mathbb{N}
, regarded as a
k
-algebra.

Every given linear operator
T:V\to V
of an
n
-dimensional
k
-module
V
is in an evident sense a matrix
[n]\times[n]\to k
, and a sequence of endomorphisms
A_{n}:W\to W
of another finite-dimensional
k
-module
W
can be regarded as a single matrix
[m]\times[m]\to\mathbb{N}\pitchfork k
; then, one can consider the linear operator
W\otimes V
, defined as

(2.43)

\sum_{n\geq 0}A_{n}\otimes T^{n}:w\otimes v\mapsto\sum_{n\geq 0}A_{n}w\otimes T^{n}v

provided the sum makes sense (either because
A_{n}
is almost-everywhere zero sequence, or because
T
is nilpotent, or because one can equip
V,W
with topologies in which (
2.43
) converges). This can be seen as an element of
\text{End}_{k}(W)\llbracket T\rrbracket
in a suitable sense. If
1\leq q,q^{\prime}\leq m
,
1\leq p,p^{\prime}\leq n
the matrix element of
\sum_{n\geq 0}A_{n}\otimes T^{n}
at the entry
((p,q),(p^{\prime},q^{\prime}))
looks precisely
\sum_{n\geq 0}(A_{n})_{qq^{\prime}}\otimes(T^{n})_{pp^{\prime}}
.

Let’s now turn to the definition of the (posetal) bicategory of 1-transducers, and conclude the section, before turning to the study of categorical properties of
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
.

Remark 2.24
.

The bicategory
\mathbf{TDX}
of 1-transducers is defined specializing the above definition to the case where the base of enrichment is the Boolean algebra
\textbf{B}=\{0,1\}
regarded as a Cartesian closed category.
\mathbf{TDX}
has

•

objects the sets
A,B,C,D,\dots
;

•

1-cells
A\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.9507pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.6886pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.3772pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 27.3772pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}B
the 1-transducers
(Q,t:A^{*}\to M(Q,\textbf{B}\langle\!\!\langle B\rangle\!\!\rangle))
, i.e. the functions of type

(2.44)

\textstyle{t:A^{*}\times Q\times Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{B}^{B^{*}}=\textbf{B}\langle\!\!\langle B\rangle\!\!\rangle}

•

2-cells
f:(P,s)\Rightarrow(Q,t)
the functions
f:P\to Q
between carriers such that

(2.45)

\forall(\underline{a},p,p^{\prime}):s(\underline{a},p,p^{\prime})\leq t(\underline{a},fp,fp^{\prime})

(the inequality has to be interpreted pointwise, or rather, as the set-theoretic inclusion of the subset of which
s(\underline{a},p,p^{\prime})
is the characteristic function into the subset of which
t(\underline{a},fp,fp^{\prime})
is the characteristic function).

Composition of 1-cells
A\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.07878pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 28.30167pt\raise 6.5pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{(P,s)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 80.19818pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 80.19818pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}B\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 32.07878pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 28.18282pt\raise 6.5pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{(Q,t)}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 80.19818pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 80.19818pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}C
is defined through the composition of relations as

(2.46)

\textstyle{A^{*}\times(P\times Q)\times(P\times Q)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(A^{*}\times P\times P)\times Q\times Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\textstyle{\textbf{B}^{B^{*}}\times Q\times Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{\textbf{B}^{C^{*}}}

where
T
is the relation induced by the universal property of
\textbf{B}^{B^{*}}
as free quantale over
B
, given that
M(Q,\textbf{B}^{C^{*}})
is a quantale.

Composition of 2-cells
P\xrightarrow{f}Q\xrightarrow{g}R
ends up being simply witnessed by the inclusion

(2.47)

\forall(\underline{a},p,p^{\prime}):r(\underline{a},p,p^{\prime})\leq s(\underline{a},fp,fp^{\prime})\leq t(\underline{a},gfp,gfp^{\prime}).

3.
Properties of
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}

The present section is the core of our work, focusing on the properties of 2-transducers as defined in
Definition-Construction 2.13
: we start by studying completeness and cocompleteness properties of 2-transducers (in
subsection 3.1
); we continue assessing the existence of monoidal structures (in
subsection 3.2
), and then we classify adjunctions (there are very few; cf.
Corollary 3.15
), while describing monads inside our bicategory (in
Definition 3.10
); lastly, we focus on the relations with other bicategories of automata (in
section 4
).

3.1.
Completeness

The existence of limits and colimits in
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
could be inferred from some of the equivalent descriptions for
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
given in
Remark 2.17
.

However, it is more natural to study a
double
category
\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}
in the way hinted at in
Remark 2.14
, as this latter presentation makes it easier to assess its cocompleteness and often (when there are tabulators in the double category of
\mathcal{V}
-profunctors) also its completeness; on the contrary, the bicategory spanned by globular cells inherits at best only lax limits and colimits of lax functors (cf.
[
GS16
, 15.18]
).

Let’s make precise the definition of such a double category, and subsequently we will proceed to construct limits and colimits in
\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}
, proving they all exist (more precisely, we construct products in
Construction 3.3
, equalizers in
Construction 3.5
; the construction of colimits suffers the complexity of colimits in
\mathbf{Cat}
: it is easy to construct coproducts in
\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}
, we do so in
Construction 3.2
; it is more difficult to exhibit a direct construction of coequalizers, but in
Construction 3.4
we prove that one can recycle the argument usually given to build colimits of profunctors, cf.
[
GP99
, §6.3]
). Cotabulators exist, as we prove in
Construction 3.6
, at least for bases of enrichment where profunctors have them; tabulators do not, and the reason is more intrinsic; in
Construction 3.7
we outline it.

Definition 3.1

(The double category of transducers)
.

The double category
\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}
of 2-transducers has

•

objects are small
\mathcal{V}
-categories
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}
, etc.;

•

a tight cell
F:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is a
\mathcal{V}
-functor;

•

a loose cell
(\mathcal{Q},t):\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 49.97168pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 59.46556pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 124.93112pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 124.93112pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{B}
is a profunctor as in
tc3
, i.e. of type

(3.1)

\textstyle{t:\mathcal{A}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\times(\mathcal{B}^{*})^{\mathrm{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{V}}

•

a cell
(U,\alpha)
with frame

(3.2)

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\scriptstyle{(\mathcal{Q},s)}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{G}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\mathcal{P},t)}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}^{\prime}}

consists of a pair where
U:\mathcal{Q}\to\mathcal{P}
is a functor and
\alpha
is a natural transformation with components

(3.3)

\textstyle{\alpha:s(a,q,q^{\prime})(b)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{t(Fa,Uq,Uq^{\prime})(Gb),}

i.e. a natural transformation filling the diagram

(3.4)

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{B}^{\mathrm{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\scriptstyle{F\times{U}^{\mathrm{op}}\times U\times G}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\alpha}
\textstyle{\mathcal{V}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{\prime}\times{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{P}\times(\mathcal{B}^{\prime})^{\mathrm{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}

The construction of some tight limits and colimits mirrors the way in which they are already constructed in the double category
\mathbb{P}\mathbf{rof}
of profunctors, cf.
[
GP99
, 6.3]
. The only subtlety arises in taking care of the grading components
\mathcal{Q}
in a family of 1-cells forming a co/cone.

In the following we sketch the construction of limits and colimits that are building blocks for all (tight, and double) co/limits, or we provide counterexamples to their existence. Moreover, from now on, we focus solely on the case where
\mathcal{V}=\mathbf{Set}
.

Construction 3.2

(Coproducts in
\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}
)
.

Coproducts in
\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}
are a bit more involved to define, as the usual recipe for building them in the double category of profunctors does not work out of the box. However, it’s easy to adapt the same argument.

Recall, for convenience, the universal property of (binary) double coproducts: given

\textstyle{(\mathcal{A}+\mathcal{C})\times{(\mathcal{P}+\mathcal{Q})}^{\mathrm{op}}\times(\mathcal{P}+\mathcal{Q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Construction 3.3

(Products in
\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}
)
.

Upon reordering the factors, from a family of 2-transducers
(\mathcal{Q}_{i},t_{i}):\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.78087pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 52.97694pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 111.95389pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 111.95389pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{B}_{i}
we obtain a 2-transducer between the products of all
\mathcal{A}_{i}
’s and
\mathcal{B}_{i}
’s,

(3.11)

\textstyle{\prod_{i:I}\mathcal{A}_{i}\times{\big(\prod_{i:I}\mathcal{Q}_{i}\big)}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\big(\prod_{i:I}\mathcal{Q}_{i}\big)\times\big(\prod_{i:I}\mathcal{B}_{i}^{*}\big)^{\mathrm{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\prod_{i:I}\mathbf{Set}}

now, post-composing with the
I
-fold Cartesian product functor
\prod_{I}:\mathbf{Set}^{I}\to\mathbf{Set}:(A_{i})\mapsto\prod_{i:I}A_{i}
, and pre-composing this with the canonical map
\big(\prod_{i:I}\mathcal{B}_{i}\big)^{*}\to\prod_{i:I}\mathcal{B}_{i}^{*}
, we obtain a 2-transducer
\prod_{I}t_{i}:\prod_{i:I}\mathcal{A}_{i}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 54.23596pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 64.79599pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 135.59198pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 135.59198pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\prod_{i:I}\mathcal{B}_{i}
. Dually to the previous construction, the universal property of double products is verified, at the level of cells, if every family of cells as on the left factors as on the right:

(3.12)

\textstyle{\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}H_{i}&\theta_{i}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}
\scriptstyle{(\mathcal{P},s)}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\hat{H}&\hat{\theta}\end{smallmatrix}\right)}

\textstyle{\mathcal{B}_{i}}

for a unique

; unwinding the type of

, this is true (when taking

to be the functors induced from

respectively) if and only if a unique cell

(3.13)

\textstyle{\hat{\theta}:s(x,p,p^{\prime})(\underline{y})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

is induced; this is the case, using the components of

as given in (

).

To conclude the discussion about colimits, we observe that one can construct reflexive coequalizers (and together with coproducts, constructed above, prove that
\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}
admits all tight colimits). A similar argument could be adapted to the existence of
general
coequalizers, at the price of handling a more complicated shape of cocone at a certain step of the proof. In short, it is simpler to construct
reflexive
coequalizers, because at some point the siftedness of the category
\{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 5.5pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.5pt\raise 3.00003pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 3.00003pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 14.5pt\raise-3.00003pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise-3.00003pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 29.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{1\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 5.50002pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\}
, although not strictly necessary, will turn out to shorten and simplify our task. This suffices to prove cocompleteness, as reflexive coequalizers and coproducts build all colimits.

\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}

; explicitly, the tabulator of

is the category

having

•

objects the triples
(a,\underline{b};\xi)
where
\xi:t(a,\bullet,\bullet)[\underline{b}]
(
\bullet
is the unique object of the category
M
);

•

arrows
(a,\underline{b};\xi)\to(x,\underline{y};\zeta)
the quadruples
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}u&v\\
m&n\end{smallmatrix}\right)
such that
u:a\to x
,

, and
m,n:M
are monoid elements, so that the two functions

(3.36)

\textstyle{{t(a,\bullet,\bullet)[\underline{b}]}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t(u,\bullet,n)[\underline{b}]}
\textstyle{t(a,\bullet,\bullet)[\underline{y}]}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces t(x,\bullet,\bullet)[\underline{y}]}
\scriptstyle{t(a,m,\bullet)[v^{*}]}
\textstyle{\xi\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\#}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\zeta}

map
\xi,\zeta
to the same element, i.e.
t(u,\bullet,n)[\underline{b}](\zeta)=t(a,m,\bullet)[v^{*}](\xi)
; the natural transformation
\tau
filling the tabulator cell sends an arrow as above into such common value in
t(a,\bullet,\bullet)[\underline{y}]=t(\text{tab}_{l}(a,\underline{b};\xi),\bullet,\bullet)[\text{tab}_{r}^{*}(x,\underline{y};\zeta)]
.

With this definition, one rotely proves a terminality property of
\tau
in the shape of (
3.34
); a span
\mathcal{A}\xleftarrow{L}\mathcal{X}\xrightarrow{R}\mathcal{B}
must define a unique functor
\mathcal{X}\to\text{tab}(M,t)
suitably applying
L,R
.

Construction 3.8

(Companions and conjoints of tight arrows in
\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}
)
.

It’s easy to observe that the embedding
\mathbb{P}\mathbf{rof}\subseteq\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}:p\mapsto\bar{p}
of
Remark 2.16
sends companions and conjoints of tight arrows
F:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
to companions and conjoints of
F
regarded as a tight arrow in
\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}
, meaning that the companion
F_{*,\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}}=:F_{\natural}
of
F
is
\overline{(F_{*,\mathbb{P}\mathbf{rof}})}
, and similarly for the conjoint.

3.2.
Monoidality

The bicategory
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
is probably a monoidal bicategory in the sense of
[
GPS95
]
, but proving all necessary coherences is a daunting task. A more sleek approach is prescribed by
[
HS19
]
, and leverages on the existence of conjoints; we are content with recording, instead, the same result for the decategorified version of
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
, the posetal bicategory of 1-transducers

(3.38)

\textstyle{t:A^{*}\times Q\times Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\text{P}({B^{*}})}

and transducer morphisms
(s,P)\to(t,Q)
of
Remark 2.24
.

The claim is that the (locally posetal) bicategory
\mathbf{TDX}
so defined is a monoidal bicategory (actually, a strict monoidal 2-category): at the best of our knowledge, even at this posetal level, this result is novel and it avoids coherence issues completely, since in a poset all diagrams commute. We will however state the definitions in the language and notation of
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
.

Remark 3.9
.

The functor
\mathbf{Set}^{(\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,)^{\mathrm{op}}}
sending a category to its presheaf category comes equipped with a tensorial strength having components

(3.39)

\textstyle{\tau:\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\times\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{Y}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}^{(\mathcal{X}^{*}\times\mathcal{Y}^{*})^{\mathrm{op}}}}

defined as
(F,G)\mapsto\big((x,y)\mapsto Fx\otimes Gy\big)
.

Composed with the functor
\Pi=\langle\pi_{\mathcal{X}}^{*},\pi_{\mathcal{Y}}^{*}\rangle:(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})^{*}\to\mathcal{X}^{*}\times\mathcal{Y}^{*}
, this yields a functor

(3.40)

\textstyle{\sigma:\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{X}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\times\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{Y}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}^{(\mathcal{X}^{*}\times\mathcal{Y}^{*})^{\mathrm{op}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,\circ\Pi}
\textstyle{\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})}\rangle\!\!\rangle}

In order to exhibit a monoidal bicategory, we have to provide:

•

a 2-functor

(3.41)

\textstyle{\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,\otimes\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,:\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}\times\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}.}

This is easy to define on objects, as
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{C})\mapsto\mathcal{A}\otimes_{0}\mathcal{C}
coincides with the product of categories
\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{C}
, and also on 1-cells, where one exploits the map

\sigma
of

. More precisely, let

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{*}\times\mathcal{C}^{*}\times(\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q})\times(\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{Q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{\sigma}

\otimes
on 1-cells.

Observe how in terms of the double cells of (

\textstyle{\mathcal{P}\times\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

This concludes the construction of the 2-functor

.

•

A two-sided unit for the
\otimes
operation; because of the way the tensor is defined, the only reasonable candidate for such a unit
i:I\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 19.00616pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 20.75807pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 47.51614pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 47.51614pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}I
is the transducer
(\{0\},i)
where
\{0\}
is a singleton state space,
I=\{\bullet\}
another singleton set, and

(3.46)

\textstyle{i:I^{*}\times\{0\}\times\{0\}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{I}\rangle\!\!\rangle}

is the map sending
(n,0,0):I^{*}\times\{0\}\times\{0\}=\mathbb{N}\times\{0\}\times\{0\}
to the singleton
\{n\}
.

The invertible modifications yielding associators and unitors are, in the posetal case, equalities, as well as the pentagonator and the two 2-unitors, hence we get a strict monoidal 2-category
(\mathbf{TDX},\otimes,I)
.

3.3.
Adjunctions and monads

Let’s start spelling out the definition of a monad in
\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}
; we will find something similar to what happens with a monad in the bicategory
\mathbf{Prof}
of profunctors, which is well-known to be a category structure; here, a grading induced by the category of states enters the picture.

Adjunctions are the following notion awaiting to be analyzed, but they will turn out to be not very interesting (cf.
Corollary 3.15
below; from this we derive that adjunctions can generate only rather uninteresting monads).

Definition 3.10

(Monad in
\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}
)
.

A monad
\Big(\mathcal{A},(\mathcal{Q},t),(\jmath,\eta),(\boxtimes,\mu)\Big)
in
\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}
consists of

•

a small category
\mathcal{A}
;

•

a 2-transducer
(\mathcal{Q},t):\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 27.26729pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.08463pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 68.16927pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 68.16927pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{A}
, i.e. a functor of type

(3.47)

\textstyle{t:\mathcal{A}^{*}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\times(\mathcal{A}^{*})^{\mathrm{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}}

•

equipped with a
unit

in
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A})

is the identity transducer on

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\times{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

where

(3.50)

\textstyle{\mu_{(\underline{u};(x,x^{\prime}),(y,y^{\prime}))}:\displaystyle\int^{\underline{a}}t(\underline{u};x,y)[\underline{a}]\times t(\underline{a},x^{\prime},y^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

•

These data are subject to unitality and associativity axioms, expressed by the commutativities of suitable diagrams (which we will display once we set up a slightly better notation, below).

The corollary that we obtain is easily stated as follows: the state category
\mathcal{Q}
part of the data that a monad in
\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}
specifies, is equipped by
(\boxtimes,\jmath)
with a monoidal structure. Furthermore, each
t(-,q,q^{\prime},-):\mathcal{A}^{*}\times(\mathcal{A}^{*})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Set}
defines a category structure on
\mathcal{A}^{*}
.
The maps in (
3.49
) in fact are given in components at the object
\underline{v}
by functions of type

\textstyle{\mu_{(\underline{u};(x,x^{\prime}),(y,y^{\prime}))}:\displaystyle\int^{\underline{a}}t(\underline{u};x,y)[\underline{a}]\otimes t(\underline{a},x^{\prime},y^{\prime})[\underline{v}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{t(\underline{u};x\boxtimes x^{\prime},y\boxtimes y^{\prime})[\underline{v}]}

and if now we denote

as

corresponds to a cowedge in

of type

(3.51)

\textstyle{t(\underline{u},\underline{a})_{xy}\otimes t(\underline{a},\underline{v})_{x^{\prime},y^{\prime}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{t(\underline{u},\underline{v})_{x\boxtimes x^{\prime},y\boxtimes y^{\prime}}}

subject to the condition that the diagram

(3.52)

\textstyle{t(\underline{u},\underline{a})_{xy}\otimes\Big(t(\underline{a},\underline{v})_{x^{\prime}y^{\prime}}\otimes t(\underline{v},\underline{w})_{x^{\prime\prime}y^{\prime\prime}}\Big)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

commutes; a similar diagram expresses the unit law asserting that the pasting of 2-cells

obtained as

(3.53)

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{*}\times{(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{I})}^{\mathrm{op}}\times(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{I})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\kern-45.0696pt\eta\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\mu}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}^{*}\times{(\mathcal{Q}\times\jmath)}^{\mathrm{op}}\times(\mathcal{Q}\times\jmath)}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{A}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{T}
\textstyle{\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{A}}\rangle\!\!\rangle}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{*}\times{(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})}^{\mathrm{op}}\times(\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{Q})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}^{*}\times{\boxtimes}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\boxtimes}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{*}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}

is the identity 2-cell of
t
(and in turn, a similar diagram expresses the other unit law
\mu\circ(\eta*t)=\text{id}_{t}
).

Packaging all together (perhaps unsurprisingly), we obtain the following result.

Theorem 3.11
.

A monad in
\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}
consists of a promonad on the category
\mathcal{A}^{*}
, so that each
t(\underline{a},q,q^{\prime},\underline{a}^{\prime})
specifies a set of
heteromorphisms
, compatible with the morphisms in
\mathcal{A}^{*}
(=finite tuples of morphisms in
\mathcal{A}
); furthermore, this promonad/category is
graded
over the state category
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}
(equipped with the pointwise monoidal structure) in the sense that composition of something ‘of degree
(x,y)
’, together with something ‘of degree
(x^{\prime},y^{\prime})
’ is of degree
(x\boxtimes x^{\prime},y\boxtimes y^{\prime})
, in the sense specified by (
3.51
).

Regarded as a functor
\mathcal{A}^{*}\to\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{A}}\rangle\!\!\rangle\text{-}\mathbf{Prof}(\mathcal{Q},\mathcal{Q})
, the transducer
t
is strong monoidal. This means, essentially, that the free monoidal structure on
\mathcal{A}^{*}
is compatible with the heteromorphisms added by the promonad
t
.

3.3.1.
Adjunctions

Characterization
tc7
of
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
in
Remark 2.17
allows for the definition of adjunctions in
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
as a special case of the definition of adjunctions in a category of a specific form; the following definition comes from
[
GKOS21
, Example 5]
, of which this is the (2-categorical) verbatim dual. Recall that a
\mathcal{M}
-graded comonad
on a category
\mathcal{C}
, consists of an oplax functor
K:\Sigma\mathcal{M}\to\mathbf{Cat}
, if
\mathcal{M}
is a monoidal category regarded as a single-object bicategory. The image under
K
of the unique object in
\Sigma\mathcal{M}
selects a category
\mathcal{C}
, and to every object of
\mathcal{M}
is associated a functor
K(M,-):\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{C}
, satisfying appropriate conditions that express its colaxity. To each such comonad one can associate a ‘locally graded’ version of the coKleisli construction.

Definition 3.12
.

The
coKleisli
\mathcal{M}
-graded category

\mathbf{coKl}^{\mathcal{M}}(K)
of a graded comonad
K
as above has

•

objects the same of
\mathcal{C}
, denoted
X,Y,Z,\dots
;

•

Now, we need a technical lemma that says that there are very few adjunctions in the coKleisli graded 2-category of the writer comonad.

Lemma 3.14
.

Let
(\mathcal{X},f):\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
and
(\mathcal{Y},g):\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
be two adjoint 1-cells in the graded coKleisli 2-category
\mathbf{coKl}^{\mathbf{Cat}}(W)
, if
W(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{A}):=\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{A}
; then it is necessary that
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{Y}=\mathcal{I}=\{\bullet\}
be the terminal category. Ultimately, an adjunction in
\mathbf{coKl}^{\mathbf{Cat}}(W)
boils down to a single adjunction
f_{\bullet}:\mathcal{A}\leftrightarrows\mathcal{B}:g_{\bullet}
in
\mathbf{Cat}
, associated to the only object of
\mathcal{I}
.

Proof.

Uncurrying
f
and
g
we obtain maps

(3.57)

\textstyle{\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}]}
\textstyle{f:\mathcal{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{[\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}]}

each of which induces a functor
f_{x}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
and
g_{y}:\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{A}
for every object
x:\mathcal{X}
and
y:\mathcal{Y}
. Unit and counit of the adjunction are given by the natural transformations filling the diagrams

(3.58)

\textstyle{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\epsilon}
\scriptstyle{f\times g}
\scriptstyle{\boldsymbol{!}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\eta}
\scriptstyle{[\text{id}_{\mathcal{A}}]}
\scriptstyle{(y_{0},x_{0})}
\textstyle{[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}]\times[\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{A}]}
\textstyle{[\mathcal{B},\mathcal{B}]}
\textstyle{[\mathcal{B},\mathcal{A}]\times[\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\circ}
\textstyle{\mathcal{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{[\text{id}_{\mathcal{B}}]}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g\times f}

so that there is a natural transformation

(3.59)

\textstyle{\eta_{a}^{y_{0}x_{0}}:a\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{g_{y_{0}}(f_{x_{0}}a)}

associated to the parameters
(y_{0},x_{0}):\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{X}
and natural in
a:\mathcal{A}
, and a counit

(3.60)

\textstyle{\epsilon_{b}^{xy}:f_{x}(g_{y}b)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{b}

which is a cocone in
(x,y):\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
and thus induces a unique
\bar{\epsilon}:\mathrm{colim}_{xy}f_{x}(g_{y}b)\to b
. Now, it’s easy to see that the triangle identities assert that

•

the composition
\textstyle{f_{x}a\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{f_{x}(g_{y_{0}}(f_{x_{0}}a))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{f_{x_{0}}a}
is equal to the identity of
f_{x}a
, and the composition

(3.61)

\textstyle{\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}\times(\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{X})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}}
\textstyle{x\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(x,y_{0},x_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{x_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
id

must be the identity of
\mathcal{X}
(recall that a 2-cell is a pair made of a functor and a natural transformation filling a triangle); thus there is only one object

in
\mathcal{X}
, and only one identity arrow.

•

Similarly, the composition
\textstyle{g_{y}b\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{g_{y_{0}}(f_{x_{0}}(g_{y}b))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{g_{y_{0}}b}
is equal to the identity of
g_{y_{0}}b
, and the composition

(3.62)

\textstyle{\mathcal{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{X})\times\mathcal{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Y}}
\textstyle{y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(x_{0},y_{0},y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{y_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
id

must be the identity of
\mathcal{Y}
.

But then, the same triangle identities imply that there is an adjunction
f_{x_{0}}\dashv g_{y_{0}}
, with unit
\eta^{x_{0}y_{0}}
and counit the map
\epsilon_{0}:f_{x_{0}}(g_{y_{0}}b)\xrightarrow{\text{in}_{(x_{0},y_{0})}}\mathrm{colim}_{xy}f_{x}(g_{y}b)\to b
. This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Corollary 3.15
.

Adjunctions in
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
are very few. Unpacking the definition of adjunction valid in any 2-category, a pair of adjoint transducers consists of

•

the left adjoint transducer
(\mathcal{X},f):\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 20.33946pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 22.42471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 50.84944pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 50.84944pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{B}
, and the right adjoint transducer
(\mathcal{Y},g):\mathcal{B}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 20.33946pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 22.42471pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 50.84944pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 50.84944pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{A}
;

•

the unit 2-cell

(3.63)

\textstyle{(H,\eta):(\mathcal{I},\iota_{\mathcal{A}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{X},g\circ f)}

consisting of a functor

(3.64)

\eta_{aa^{\prime}}:\mathcal{A}^{*}(\underline{a}^{\prime},\underline{a})\to G(f(\underline{a},x_{0},x_{0}),y_{0},y_{0})[\underline{a}^{\prime}].

The unit map, together with the counit, provide an adjunction
f_{x_{0}x_{0}}=f(-,x_{0},x_{0})\dashv g(-,y_{0},y_{0})=g_{y_{0}y_{0}}
.

4.
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
and other bicategories of automata

The scope of this section is to compare
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
with other ‘bicategories of processes’, most importantly the bicategory of
circuits
studied by Walters et al. in
[
KSW97
]
and the bicategory
\mathbf{MAC}
of
machines
described by Guitart
[
Gui74
]
.

4.1.
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
and bicategories of circuits

We start by recalling the definition of the bicategory
\mathbf{Mly}(\mathcal{K})
of
Mealy automata
valued in a Cartesian category
\mathcal{K}
.
16
16

16

We will be particularly interested in two cases:
\mathcal{K}
equal to the category of sets and functions, and the 1-category of categories and functors; generalizing this picture is possible (the special case of a topos as Cartesian closed category was studied in
[
Hor24
]
, and
[
Gui74
]
takes into account some features of
\mathbf{Cat}
qua 2-category, not just 1-category.) but out of the scope of the present section, i.e. building a comparison functor
\mathbf{Mly}(\mathcal{K})\to\mathbf{TDX}
and
\mathbf{Mly}(\mathbf{Cat})\to\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
.
The construction that follows comes from
[
KSW10
,
KSW97
]
.

Let
\mathbb{N}
denote the one-object, 2-discrete bicategory on the graph with one vertex and one edge (it’s ‘the monoidal category of natural numbers’: hence the name). For every bicategory
\mathcal{B}
, define
\Omega\mathcal{B}
as the bicategory of pseudofunctors
A:\mathbb{N}\to\mathcal{B}
, lax natural transformations between such functors, and modifications between such lax natural transformations. Let
\Sigma\mathcal{K}
denote the monoidal category
(\mathcal{K},\times,1)
, regarded as a single-object bicategory.

Definition 4.1

(The bicategory of Mealy automata)
.

The
bicategory of Mealy automata
, denoted as
\mathbf{Mly}(\mathcal{K})
, is defined as
\Omega(\Sigma\mathcal{K})
.

Explicitly,

ml
1)

an object of
\mathbf{Mly}(\mathcal{K})
consists of a mapping sending the unique object of
\mathbb{N}
to the unique object
\bullet
of
\Sigma\mathcal{K}
; the unique morphism generator of
\mathbb{N}
then picks an object
A:\mathcal{K}
, together with all its iterates
A^{\times n}
(image of
n:\mathbb{N}
).

ml
2)

A 1-cell of
\mathbf{Mly}(\mathcal{K})
is a
Mealy automaton
, i.e. a tuple
(E,d,s)
where
E:\mathcal{K}
is an object of ‘states’ (witnessing the unique component of a lax natural transformation between two pseudofunctors
A,B:\mathbb{N}\to\Sigma\mathcal{K}
), and a laxity cell
\langle d,s\rangle:A\times X\to X\times B
, or rather a span

(4.1)

\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{A\times X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{B.}

Note how
\Omega(\Sigma(\mathcal{K},\otimes,I))
makes sense for every monoidal category, but this span representation exists only if
\mathcal{K}
is Cartesian.

ml
3)

A 2-cell
f:(X,d,s)\Rightarrow(Y,d^{\prime},s^{\prime})
, i.e. a modification, consists of a map between state spaces,
f:X\to Y
such that the following diagram commutes:

(4.2)

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{A\times X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s}
\scriptstyle{d}
\scriptstyle{A\times f}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y}
\textstyle{A\times Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{d^{\prime}}
\textstyle{B.}

Composition of spans like

(4.3)

\textstyle{X}
\textstyle{A\times X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{d^{\prime}}
\textstyle{C}

consists of the pasting of lax natural transformations, which when destructured appears as a composite span

(4.4)

\textstyle{X\times Y}
\textstyle{A\times(X\times Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{d^{\prime}\diamond d}
\textstyle{C}

where

and
