are given respectively by (given in the category of sets for convenience, but it’s easy to lambda-abstract the definition to work in any Cartesian
\mathcal{K}
)

(4.5)

\begin{cases}d^{\prime}\diamond d(a,(x,y))=\big(d(\underline{a},x),d^{\prime}(s(\underline{a},x),y)\big),\\
s^{\prime}\diamond s(a,(x,y))=s^{\prime}(s(\underline{a},x),y).\end{cases}

Remark 4.2
.

Fixed two objects
A,B:\mathcal{K}
, the hom-category
\mathbf{Mly}(A,B)
enjoys several universal properties:

mp
1)

if
\mathcal{K}
is monoidal closed, the hom-category
\mathbf{Mly}(A,B)
is isomorphic the category of coalgebras for the endofunctor

(4.6)

\textstyle{R_{AB}:\mathcal{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{K}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{[A,B\times X].}

As a consequence, there exists an inserter diagram

(4.7)

\textstyle{\mathbf{Mly}(A,B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\textstyle{\mathcal{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{R_{AB}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{K}.}

mp
2)

the hom-category
\mathbf{Mly}(A,B)
fits in the strict 2-pullback

(4.8)

\textstyle{\mathbf{Mly}(A,B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(A\times\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,/B)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{U}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Alg}(A\times\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{U^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{K},}

where
\mathbf{Alg}(A\times\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,)
is the category of endofunctor algebras for
A\times-:\mathcal{K}\to\mathcal{K}
, and
(A\times-/B)
the comma category of arrows
A\times X\to B
and
U,U^{\prime}
are forgetful functors.

Similarly to what happens with the numerous characterizations of
Remark 2.17
, it is sometimes more convenient, depending on the kind of problem at hand, to argue with the bare definition of
\mathbf{Mly}(A,B)
, or with one of the characterizations

or

above.

Now, our purpose in the present section is to build a comparison functor

(4.9)

\textstyle{\mathbb{G}:\mathbf{Mly}(\mathbf{Cat})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}}

thus specializing the construction of
\mathbf{Mly}(\mathcal{K})
to the case
\mathcal{K}=\mathbf{Cat}
, and then to the case
\mathcal{K}=\mathbf{Set}
to obtain a functor

(4.10)

\textstyle{G:\mathbf{Mly}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{TDX}}

from the bicategory
\mathbf{Mly}=\mathbf{Mly}(\mathbf{Set})
, to the bicategory of 1-transducers of
Remark 2.24
.
First of all, we define
\mathbb{G}
to be the identity on objects; then, we observe that any Mealy automaton
(\mathcal{X},d,s):\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
between categories induces a functor

(4.11)

\textstyle{D:\mathcal{A}^{*}\times\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{B}^{*}}

in the following way:

•

the functor
d:\mathcal{A}^{*}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}
clearly has an extension to
d^{*}:\mathcal{A}^{*}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{X}
, given the universal property of the
\mathcal{A}^{*}
;

•

the functor
s:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{B}^{*}\to\mathcal{B}
has an extension to
s^{*}:\mathcal{A}^{*}\times\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{B}^{*}
, defined ‘inductively’ (cf.
[
BLLL23a
, Lemma 2.13]
) as

(4.12)

\begin{cases}s^{\flat}(e,\texttt{[\,]})&=\texttt{[\,]}\\
s^{\flat}(e,a\mathbin{::}as)&=s(e,a)\mathbin{::}s^{\flat}(d(e,a),as).\end{cases}

Now, let
D:=\langle d^{*},s^{\flat}\rangle
, and note that the representable profunctor
\hom(1,D):\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{X}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 22.91748pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 25.6473pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 57.29459pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 57.29459pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{B}
defined by
D
and given by

(4.13)

\big((x^{\prime},\underline{b}),(x,\underline{a})\big)\mapsto\mathcal{X}(x^{\prime},d(\underline{a},x))\times\mathcal{B}^{*}(b,s^{\flat}(\underline{a},x))

has precisely the correct type for being a 2-transducer
(\mathcal{X},\hom(1,D)):\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 22.91748pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 25.6473pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 57.29459pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 57.29459pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{B}
. This defined
\mathbb{G}
on 1-cells, so that it is a (pseudo)functor: it preserves identities, as the Mealy automaton

(4.14)

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{*}\times\mathcal{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{\mathcal{I}\times\mathcal{A}^{*}}

is sent to the transducer defined as

\displaystyle\big((\bullet,\underline{a}^{\prime}),(\underline{a},\bullet)\big)

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathcal{I}(\bullet,d^{*}(\underline{a},\bullet))\times\mathcal{A}^{*}(\underline{a}^{\prime},s^{\flat}(\underline{a},\bullet))

\displaystyle\cong I\times\mathcal{A}^{*}(\underline{a}^{\prime},\underline{a})

\displaystyle\cong\mathcal{A}^{*}(\underline{a}^{\prime},\underline{a})

It preserves compositions, as given transducers

(4.15)

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{G}(\mathcal{X},d_{1},s_{1})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{G}(\mathcal{Y},d_{2},s_{2})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{C}}

their composition
qua
transducers is given by

(4.16)

\big((\underline{a},(x,y)),((x^{\prime},y^{\prime}),\underline{c})\big)\longmapsto\\
\int^{\underline{b}}\mathcal{X}\big(x^{\prime},d_{1}^{*}(\underline{a},x)\big)\times\mathcal{B}^{*}\big(b,s_{1}^{\flat}(\underline{a},x)\big)\times\mathcal{Y}\big(y^{\prime},d_{2}^{*}(\underline{b},y)\big)\times\mathcal{C}^{*}\big(\underline{c},s_{2}^{\flat}(\underline{b},y)\big)

which can be reduced by a straightforward calculation to

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}(\mathcal{Y},d_{2},s_{2})\circ\mathbb{G}(\mathcal{X},d_{1},s_{1})

\displaystyle\cong\kern 2.84526pt

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}\big(x^{\prime},d_{1}^{*}(\underline{a},x)\big)\times\int^{\underline{b}}\mathcal{B}^{*}\big(b,s_{1}^{\flat}(\underline{a},x)\big)\times\mathcal{Y}\big(y^{\prime},d_{2}^{*}(\underline{b},y)\big)\times\mathcal{C}^{*}\big(\underline{c},s_{2}^{\flat}(\underline{b},y)\big)

\displaystyle\cong\kern 2.84526pt

\displaystyle\mathcal{X}\big(x^{\prime},d_{1}^{*}(\underline{a},x)\big)\times\mathcal{Y}\big(y^{\prime},d_{2}^{*}(s_{1}^{\flat}(\underline{a},x),y)\big)\times\mathcal{C}^{*}\big(\underline{c},s_{2}^{\flat}(s_{1}^{\flat}(\underline{a},x),y)\big)

\displaystyle\cong\kern 2.84526pt

\displaystyle(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})\big((x^{\prime},y^{\prime}),(d_{1}^{*}(\underline{a},x),d_{2}^{*}(s_{1}^{\flat}(\underline{a},x),y))\big)\times\mathcal{C}^{*}\big(\underline{c},s_{2}^{\flat}(\underline{b},y)\big)

\displaystyle=\kern 2.84526pt

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y},d_{2}\diamond d_{1},s_{2}\diamond s_{1})\big((\underline{a},(x,y)),((x^{\prime},y^{\prime}),\underline{c})\big).

On 2-cells, every functor
F:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{Y}
such that the squares

(4.17)

\textstyle{\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{s_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}\times F}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Y}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{2}}
\scriptstyle{s_{2}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}}

both commute is also that

(4.18)

\begin{cases}s_{2}^{\flat}(\underline{a},Fx)=s_{1}^{\flat}(\underline{a},x),\\
F(d_{1}^{*}(\underline{a},x))=d_{2}^{*}(\underline{a},Fx).\end{cases}

So, there is an induced 2-cell between the associated transducers, mediated by the functorial action of
F
and the embedding

: as a consequence there is a pseudocommutative square of profunctors

(4.19)

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{*}\times\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{B}^{*}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}^{*}\times\mathcal{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{B}^{*}}

whence a transformation with components induced by the action of
F
,

\displaystyle((x^{\prime},\underline{b}),(\underline{a},x))

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathbb{G}(D)((x^{\prime},\underline{b}),(\underline{a},x))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{X}(x^{\prime},d_{1}(\underline{a},x))\times\mathcal{B}^{*}(\underline{b},s_{1}^{\flat}(\underline{a},x))

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{Y}(Fx^{\prime},Fd_{1}(\underline{a},x))\times\mathcal{B}^{*}(\underline{b},s_{1}^{\flat}(\underline{a},x))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{Y}(Fx^{\prime},d_{2}(\underline{a},Fx))\times\mathcal{B}^{*}(\underline{b},s_{1}^{\flat}(\underline{a},Fx))

\displaystyle=\mathbb{G}(D^{\prime})((Fx^{\prime},\underline{b}),(\underline{a},Fx))

and filling the triangle

(4.20)

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\times{\mathcal{X}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\kern 4.09723pt\theta_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}\times{F}^{\mathrm{op}}\times F}
\scriptstyle{\mathbb{G}(D)}
\textstyle{\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\times{\mathcal{Y}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Y}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Everything that has been done for
\mathbf{Set}
-enriched categories can be specialized to discrete ones, regarded as enriched over Booleans; this yields the 2-functor

of (

).

Remark 4.3
.

Summing up the results of this subsection, we have constructed a diagram

(4.21)

\textstyle{\mathbf{Cat}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi}
\scriptstyle{J}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Mly}(\mathbf{Cat})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{I}
\textstyle{\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}}

commutative up to iso, where the composite functor
\Pi=IJ
is a proarrow equipment, yet
I
and
J
separately are not proarrow equipments, since

•

not every 1-cell in the image of
J
has a conjoint; in fact, only the companions of isomorphisms of categories admit a conjoint in
\mathbf{Mly}(\mathbf{Cat})
;

•

not every 1-cell in the image of
I
has a conjoint: in order to have a conjoint it is necessary to have terminal category of states. But then, every 1-cell in
\mathbf{Mly}(\mathbf{Cat})
, in the image of
J
, has a conjoint when mapped through
I
.

4.2.
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
and Guitart’s
\mathbf{MAC}

The relation of
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
with another notable bicategory of ‘machines’ is more subtle; let’s start recalling the definition of the bicategory
\mathbf{MAC}
of ‘machines’ that René Guitart gave in
[
Gui74
, §2]
. We are basically repeating an outline of Guitart’s definition that appears in
[
BLLL23a
]
.

Definition 4.4
.

\mathbf{MAC}
is the bicategory having

•

objects the small categories
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B},\dots
;

•

as hom-categories
\mathbf{Mac}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
the full subcategory of
\mathbf{Span}(\mathbf{Cat})(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})
spanned by diagrams

(4.22)

\textstyle{\mathcal{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}}

where
q
is a discrete opfibration.

We informally refer to 1-cells of
\mathbf{MAC}
as ‘machines’.

Recall from
[
Jay88
]
that a
local adjunction
between bicategories
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y}
consists of lax functors
L:\mathcal{X}\rightleftarrows\mathcal{Y}:R
equipped with a family of adjunctions

(4.23)

\lambda_{XY}:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 24.53473pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-24.53473pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{Y}(LX,Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 48.53473pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 34.12587pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\perp}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern 48.53473pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathcal{X}(X,RY)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.53473pt\raise-5.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces:\rho_{XY}

By evident extension of this terminology, a
local reflection
is a local adjunction where each
\rho_{XY}
is fully faithful. The following adapts a well-known fact about profunctors represented as spans.

Lemma 4.5
.

There is a local reflection

(4.24)

(\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,)^{\varphi}:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 16.79854pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-16.79854pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbf{MAC}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 40.79854pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.73952pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\perp}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern 40.79854pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathbf{Prof}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 16.79854pt\raise-5.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces:j

where the bicategory on the right hand side is profunctors, regarded as two-sided discrete fibrations, inside
\mathbf{Span}(\mathbf{Cat})
.

The purpose of the present subsection is to prove the following result.

Theorem 4.6
.

There is a local reflection

(4.25)

\Rbag\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 18.47214pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-18.47214pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 42.47214pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 25.57631pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\perp}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern 42.47214pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathbf{Prof}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 18.47214pt\raise-5.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces:\boldsymbol{u}\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,

induced by the span representation of profunctors.

Proof.

For every
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}:\mathbf{Cat}
we have to define a reflection

(4.26)

(\Rbag\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,)_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}}:\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 31.04161pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-31.04161pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 55.04161pt\raise 5.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 39.2951pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\perp}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\kern 55.04161pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\mathbf{Prof}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B}^{*})}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 31.04163pt\raise-5.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces:(\boldsymbol{u}\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,)_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{B}}.

We define

•

\Rbag\big((\mathcal{Q},t):\mathcal{A}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.76427pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 35.45592pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 76.91185pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 76.91185pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{B}\big)
as the machine obtained from a 2-transducer
(\mathcal{Q},t)
as follows:

–

first, take the colimit
\int t=\mathrm{colim}_{{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}}\big(t(\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,,q,q)\big):\mathcal{A}\times(\mathcal{B}^{*})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Set}
(not the coend, see below);

–

then, turn
\int t
into a two-sided discrete fibration

(4.27)

\textstyle{\mathcal{E}(\int t)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{t}}
\scriptstyle{q_{t}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{B}^{*},}

using the well-known equivalence of categories.

•

\boldsymbol{u}(\mathcal{E};q,p)
as the 2-transducer obtained from a two-sided discrete fibration regarding it as a profunctor
\mathcal{A}\times(\mathcal{B}^{*})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathbf{Set}
, and thus as a transducer

(4.28)

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\times{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{I}(\mathcal{B}^{*})^{\mathrm{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Set}.}

Now start with a transducer
(\mathcal{Q},t)
and apply
\boldsymbol{u}
to
\Rbag(\mathcal{Q},t)=(\int t,p_{t},q_{t})
; then there is a transformation with components

(4.29)

\textstyle{\eta:(\mathcal{Q},t)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\boldsymbol{u}\big(\Rbag(\mathcal{Q},t)\big)}

in turn having components

(4.30)

\textstyle{\eta^{2}:t(\underline{a},q,q^{\prime})(\underline{b})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{colim}_{qq^{\prime}}t(\underline{a},q,q^{\prime})(\underline{b})}

induced by the initial cocone defining
\int t
(defining it as the coend would have given just a cowedge here, not a natural transformation), and giving rise to a 2-cell in
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}
of shape

(4.31)

\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\eta^{2}}
\textstyle{\textbf{K}\langle\!\!\langle{\mathcal{B}}\rangle\!\!\rangle}
\textstyle{\mathcal{A}\times{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{I}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

yielding the desired unit of the reflection; the counit is indeed an isomorphism, as the composition
\Rbag\big(\boldsymbol{u}(\mathcal{E};q,g)\big)
consists of starting with a two-sided discrete fibration, regard it as a profunctor, and revert it back to a two-sided discrete fibration.

In a fibrational perspective, we are identifying
\mathbf{Prof}
with the fiber at
\mathcal{I}
of a functor
\mathbf{2}\mathbf{TDX}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{B})\to\mathbf{Cat}:(\mathcal{Q},\,\rule{5.0pt}{0.4pt}\,)\mapsto\mathcal{Q}
; this fiber is reflective.
∎

5.
Future plans

We end the paper sketching two possible directions for future investigation. Admittedly, some parts of it remain to this day a bit conjectural, but we believe there is some deep observation lurking behind them, that deserves mention, and can further convince the reader that categorification allowed to unveil an interesting pattern.

5.1.
A more general double category of transducers

Along the discussion so far, we studied transducers between free objects, in an attempt to retain some intuition about the classical theory of processes as representations of a free monoid on an alphabet.

There have been attempts (cf. the former Guitart approach in
[
Gui74
]
) where such a restraint was abandoned, considering processes between non-free monoids. We would like to study where this idea goes, and define a
double category
of transducers between general monoidal categories. Part of the motivation for doing so is the same as Guitart’s: most of the results outlined so far extend without much effort, i.e. they are completely independent on the freeness assumption on the domain and codomain of a 2-transducer, but the embedding result of
\mathbf{Mly}(\mathbf{Cat})
relies on a more refined compatibility between the output map
S
of a span

(5.1)

\textstyle{\mathcal{X}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{M}\times\mathcal{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{S}
\scriptstyle{D}
\textstyle{\mathcal{N},}

namely the property that there is a natural isomorphism in
\mathcal{N}
,

(5.2)

\textstyle{S(M,X)\otimes S(M\ltimes X,M^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{XMM^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{S(M\otimes M^{\prime},X)}

subject to suitable compatibility conditions.

Another reason to indulge in such a maximal level of generality is that this refined notion, albeit burdening the analysis with a lot of new coherence problems, is neither new nor artificial. It first appeared in
[
Gui74
]
without particular explanation besides its utility, and was further developed in
[
BLLL23a
, §2.1]
, where the condition was termed the
fugal property
for
S
.
17
17

17

Guitart does not give a name to this property; in
[
BLLL23a
]
we call it
fugality
for
s
(cf. [
ibi
] for a thorough description of the matter); it can be motivated with the fact that this requirement on
s
is necessary and sufficient for
s
to induce a functor
\mathcal{E}[d^{+}]\to B
, and thus a machine, where
\mathcal{E}[d^{+}]
is the domain of the discrete opfibration over
A^{*}
naturally associated to the representation
d^{+}
.
The same notion was further studied (and in large part mechanized in a proof-assistant) in
[
Lor25
, Definition 2.18]
, finding that in a double category modeled on
\mathbf{Mly}(\mathbf{Set})
, a monad map between monads
M
and
N
is exactly equivalent to a third Mealy automaton between the state spaces of
M
and
N
, whose output function satisfies the fugal property. What may initially appear to be an
ad hoc
modification of standard definitions, therefore, arises instead quite naturally from first principles.

The study of a categorified notion of fugality is part of a larger work in progress, taking its initial steps from the observation that

•

there is a larger bicategory
\mathcal{V}\mathbf{Mealy}
where usual Mealy automata embed, outlined by Paré in his
[
Par10
]
; such a bicategory seems the most suitable candidate for a ‘bicategory of general automata’ as it is equipped with a clear-cut universal property and extends (in the sense that there are embedding from the latter into the former) virtually all examples, such as
[
KSW97
,
Gui74
]
of bicategories of automata;

•

the pseudo double category having
\mathcal{V}\mathbf{Mealy}
as bicategory of loose arrows contains (via a local coreflection, cf.
[
Jay88
]
, the notion has been exploited in
[
BLLL23a
, Lemma 2.13]
) all the double categories outlined here, the double category of ‘circuits’ outlined in
[
Lor25
]
, as well as the double categorical analogue of Guitart’s
\mathbf{MAC}
.

Definition 5.1

(The double category of transducers between monoidal categories)
.

The
double category of monoidal transducers

\mathbb{M}\mathbb{D}\mathbf{TDX}
is defined as follows:

•

0-cells
are monoidal categories
\mathcal{M},\mathcal{N},\dots
;

•

a tight cell
F:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
is a strong monoidal functor between the underlying categories of
\mathcal{M}
and
\mathcal{N}
;

•

a loose cell
(\mathcal{Q},t):\mathcal{M}\mathrel{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 45.05287pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\lx@xy@point}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 53.31696pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 112.63391pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 112.63391pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces}\mathcal{N}
are pairs where
\mathcal{Q}
is a category and
t
a functor of type

(5.3)

\textstyle{t:\mathcal{M}\times{\mathcal{Q}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathcal{Q}\times\mathcal{N}^{\mathrm{op}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{V}}

•

a cell
(u,\alpha)
with frame

(5.4)

\textstyle{\mathcal{M}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

5.2.
More on the linear algebra, and representation theory, of profunctors

The mention of ‘structure constants’ in
Remark 2.10
gives some leeway to speculate on the theory of transducers from a representation-theoretic point of view; in particular, what is their geometric meaning when they are interpreted as categorified matrices?

If in
Definition 2.7
the base semiring
\mathbb{K}
is taken to be a field (better, algebraically closed of characteristic zero), a 1-transducer as in (
1.1
) consists precisely of a representation of the free monoid
A^{*}
into the Lie algebra of
n\times n
matrices valued in Laurent series over
\mathbb{K}
.

One classical context where such Laurent-series-valued matrices arise is the study of linear ODEs with regular singularities, and especially meromorphic connections on vector bundles
[
Was65
,
Lev71
,
Del70
,
Mal91
,
Boa01
,
Sab07
]
, a well-established area of algebro-geometric approaches to the analysis of ODEs.

In short: when given a differential equation of type

(5.7)

Y^{\prime}(z)=A(z)Y(z)

where
A(z)\in\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{n}(\mathbb{C}(\!(z)\!))

one attempts to classify its solutions looking at the structure of
A
, thought as a matrix of germs of meromorphic functions. If, say,
A(z)
has ‘order 1’, i.e. it admits an expansion
\frac{A_{-1}}{z}+A_{0}+A_{1}z+\dots
then the constant matrix
A_{-1}
describes the
monodromy
of the differential equation. For higher orders, the equation has
irregular
singularities the classification of which requires refined (highly nontrivial) tools.

Motivated by this perspective, we wonder what is the theory of transducers from a ‘Lie-theoretic perspective’; for example, the structure constants
(t_{a}\mid a\in A)
of
Remark 2.10
uniquely determine
t
due to the freeness property of
A^{*}
; one can study the subset of
A
yielding pairwise-commuting
t_{a}
’s (under composition of matrices/profunctors); the elements of such a set are simultaneously diagonalizable, a property which turns out to be a cornerstone integrable systems theory
[
Kos79
,
BBT03
]
.

Or, again over the field of Laurent series with complex coefficients, any matrix-valued function
\Phi(z)
in
\mathfrak{g}\mathfrak{l}_{n}(K)
gives rise to a
spectral curve

[
Bea95
,
DM96
]
, defined by means of the characteristic equation
\det(\lambda I-\Phi(z))=0
. This allows the study of the parametric family of operators given by
\Phi
in terms of line bundles over the spectral curve.

To each 1-transducer
t
one can associate the function
C_{t}:A\to K[\lambda]
so that
C_{t}(a)=\det(\lambda I-t_{a}(z))
, and study the locus of
z
for which
C_{t}(a)
vanishes, be it as a mere subset or as a subspace.
18
18

18

For example if
A
is a topological space, the list construction yields a topological monoid
A^{*}

[
GM19
]
, of which the
commutant

(5.8)

\mathrm{Comm}(A):=\left\{a\in A\mid[t_{a},t_{b}]=0\text{ for all }b\in A\right\}.

is a closed subspace.

We leave the suggestive open question of whether there is something to gain from this formal analogy, and what are the interesting ‘Lie theoretic’ invariants to attach a 1-transducer, open for future investigation.
